#126. Witnesses recount struggle to save people buried in ‘horrifying’ Gaza landslide

High winds and heavy rain triggered collapse of sand bank overlooking camp for displaced families

Witnesses have described the frantic struggle to save three members of a family buried by a landslide triggered by high winds and heavy rain in Gaza.

A sand bank overlooking a camp for displaced families south of Deir al-Balah collapsed on Sunday, killing five people and crushing at least three tents.

“It was a horrifying sight,” said Abdul Karim al-Sahhar, 30, originally from Izbat Beit Hanoun in northern Gaza. “We saw people buried beneath the sand and rocks. None of them was moving or making a sound. Even the tent had disappeared completely. There was no trace of it left.”

Among the casualties were an eight-year-old, a six-year-old and their mother.

Sahhar, whose own shelter was badly damaged in the landslide, said the children’s father was outside the family tent and so survived. “One of his children had called him to come and eat with them,” he said. “He was a little late returning, and then the collapse happened.

“The father started shouting: ‘Save my children! Save my children!’ People came and started digging [but] there was a huge boulder on top of the victims. The young men had to break it into pieces so they could reach the people buried underneath.”

Abu Darbali, another witness, said the collapse of the embankment sounded like an explosion. “We found that three tents were covered completely after the mountain collapsed on top of them,” he told Reuters. “People were under the rubble and we couldn’t see them.”

Video posted to social media showed people using hammers to break large rocks and others removing sand with shovels, using flashlights to search for those trapped underneath.

The UN said more than 26,000 households had reported damage to shelters or belongings after the weekend’s rainstorms.

The vast majority of Gaza’s population of 2.3 million live in tent encampments or the ruins of bombed-out buildings, ‌with reconstruction yet to begin after Israel’s relentless two-year military assault launched after the Hamas attacks on southern Israel in October 2023.

“The collapse [this weekend] was waiting to happen. [Gaza] is extremely overcrowded. People are just trying to find a place wherever,” said Toby Fricker, a spokesperson for Unicef, which has been working to prevent similar tragedies and improve drainage in camps for displaced people.

In a statement, the Hamas-run government media office said about “70,000 tents sheltering tens of thousands of displaced people were flooded on the first day” of rains. It also noted the “collapse of dozens of cracked walls and roofs, which caused injuries”, some critical, in various areas of the tiny territory.

A US-brokered ceasefire last year called for the eventual reconstruction of Gaza, but little progress has been made. Negotiators have failed to agree on terms for Hamas to disarm or for Israel to withdraw its troops, which control about two-thirds of the territory ‌from which residents have been ordered to leave.

Israel says no reconstruction can happen before Hamas is disarmed.

Sahhar said he and his family were now homeless. “My tent and everything I own are now buried under the sand. My mattress, household utensils and all my belongings – everything was buried. I could not retrieve anything,” he said.

Mustafa Qazzaat, a member of the Gaza City council, said Israel had not allowed prefabricated homes to shelter the displaced since the truce was announced, and only permitted the entry of limited numbers of tents.

Many so-called “dual use” items remain restricted, such as metal tent poles. Humanitarian workers said they instead had used wooden poles fashioned from palettes used to transport food.

Cogat, the Israeli defence ministry body that oversees aid to Gaza, said more than 91,000 tents and over 8,200 trucks containing tarpaulins, blankets and winter clothing had reached Gaza, as well as convoys carrying supplies of fuel and engine oil to power pumping facilities and other humanitarian installations.

Earlier this year, an assessment compiled by shelter specialists in Gaza revealed that thousands of tents supplied by China, Egypt and Saudi Arabia to shelter displaced Palestinians offered only limited protection against rain and wind.

#123. Russia's leading independent newspaper closes after eviction

Russia's leading independent newspaper has closed after being evicted from its offices and facing pressure from authorities.

For 30 years, Novaya Gazeta published investigations into corruption, human rights violations and abuse of power in the country.

On Monday, the team behind the newspaper had a final editorial meeting before packing up their belongings and leaving.

The Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said Russia's media was highly diverse and that the newspaper's closure was part of "a standard rotational process".

The BBC spoke to its co-founder Dmitry Muratov, a Nobel Peace Prize winner, about the closure.

#122. New Knesset speaker could be key to ousting Netanyahu as Israel’s PM

Opposition politicians get first chance to take control of levers of power, even as they struggle to form government

Israel’s new Knesset will be sworn in on Tuesday, giving opposition politicians their first chance to take control of some levers of power, even as they struggle to form a new government to unseat Benjamin Netanyahu.

High on the agenda will be replacing the speaker, who wields immense influence by controlling the legislature’s agenda and committee chairs. The current speaker is Amir Ohana, a close ally of Netanyahu.

He will be replaced by Matan Kahana, from the Yashar party led by Gadi Eisenkot, the former Israeli military chief who got the presidential mandate to try to form a coalition government at the weekend.

Kahana, a former minister, fighter pilot and special forces officer, has the explicit backing of all four Jewish Israeli parties in Eisenkot’s “change” bloc. The two other Palestinian-led opposition parties have said replacing Ohana is a priority so are expected to give Kahana their votes.

The opposition parties hold 67 seats in the 120-seat legislature, enough to install a consensus choice for speaker. They will request a vote on Tuesday afternoon, and Ohana then has up to a week to bring it to the floor.

Avigdor Lieberman, who heads another opposition party, is nominated to head the foreign affairs and defence committee. It will play a central role in drawing up planned legislation covering the conscription of ultra-Orthodox men, a key campaign issue for Lieberman.

The effort to wrest away control of the Knesset is significant enough that more than 160 former top security officials, including heads of the military, intelligence and police, warned at the weekend that rightwing activists were planning to disrupt the day with violent attacks.

“Actors who oppose replacing the Knesset speaker intend to go to the Knesset on the day of the swearing-in ceremony and to prevent the MKs and their family members from entering the building,” a forum of retired commanders said in a letter to serving security chiefs.

“These same actors are encouraging tens of thousands of citizens who support their stance to come and to help them with their mission,” Ma’ariv newspaper quoted the letter as saying.

All opposition parties have described Netanyahu as a threat to the state and are keen to start moving against him in the Knesset, despite divides blocking the formation of a coalition.

Several of the Jewish-Israeli opposition parties in Eisenkot’s bloc have sworn not to sit with the Palestinian-led parties, which hold more than a quarter of the bloc’s seats, or even join a minority government that has their backing.

Eisenkot has vowed to broker a national emergency government, promising to achieve it with “creative and unconventional political acts”.

In a sign of challenges ahead, he effectively warned that some election promises would have to be broken to unseat Netanyahu. “No one will be able to fulfil all the [campaign] promises they made, and all of us will need to compromise. That is also leadership, to know when to decide and set priorities,” he said.

There have been protests across Israel urging Jewish-Israeli opposition parties to put unseating Netanyahu ahead of a racist commitment to excluding Palestinian-led parties from government.

Palestinian citizens of Israel make up about 20% of the population and voted in unprecedented numbers this year, giving the parties 19 seats. Without them, the opposition bloc would have fewer seats than Netanyahu’s diminished coalition.

Lieberman has been trying to seek an alternative path to government through an alliance with ultra-Orthodox parties.

They have long been Netanyahu allies but have been frustrated by his failure to deliver on the promise to pass broad military draft exemptions for their community into law. Lieberman has campaigned hard for universal conscription, but appears to prefer compromise on this issue to working with Palestinians.

“Has the End of Times arrived?” the leading Haredi (ultra-Orthodox) journalist Arye Erlich wrote in a column for Yedioth Ahronoth newspaper.

“Has Avigdor Lieberman … the biggest persecutor of Haredim Israeli politics has seen in decades, suddenly come to believe that he can compromise on a military draft bill and sit in a coalition government alongside the Haredim?”

Even if Eisenkot does broker a coalition deal, he may delay announcing it until Netanyahu’s speaker has been replaced. Netanyahu is a master of political survival and last-minute deals, fracturing past coalitions by luring defectors into his own alliance.

Ohana could delay signing in a new government for up to a week, and some of the opposition do not want to give Netanyahu that much time to attack the pressure points of any fragile deal.

If Eisenkot cannot form a government, the mandate is passed to other leaders, and if they fail, Israel must hold elections again. In the meantime Netanyahu and his far-right allies would stay on as a caretaker government.

Although Netanyahu is calling for his opponents to join him in a unity government, a long period of limbo followed by a new vote offers him a likelier path back to power.

If an alternative government took power, it would be harder for Netanyahu to destabilise it, said Gideon Rahat, a chair in political science at the Hebrew University of Jerusalem.

“Once they establish a government, they might be a minority government, it might be hard for them to do things, but nevertheless, they will be governing and it would be hard to oust them.”

#121. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#117. EU manufacturers could give up on competing with China by 2030, warns commerce chief

Jens Eskelund says Europe may need to allow its companies to become part of Chinese firms’ supply chains

Europe’s manufacturers could give up on attempting to compete with their Chinese counterparts by the end of the decade if current trends continue amid fears over mass job losses, the head of the European Union Chamber of Commerce in China has warned.

In a case of “if you can’t beat them, join them”, Jens Eskelund said European companies operating in China were now thinking “Made in China for China” could be replaced by a philosophy of “Made in China for the world”.

Eskelund, who heads the influential Beijing-headquartered body, said European manufacturers could find themselves at an existential crossroads by the end of the decade, asking themselves: “What is the point of trading?”

As the EU gets tough with China with a deficit running at a record £1bn (€1.18bn) a day and an increasing mood for protectionism in Brussels, Berlin and Paris, he said European countries might need to shift their policies to allow domestic manufacturers to become part of Chinese firms’ supply chains, rather than treat them as competitors.

Eskelund, who is also a managing director of the shipping group Maersk’s Chinese operations, pointed to a report by Goldman Sachs that forecasted Chinese exports would lower the EU’s gross domestic product by 0.5% by the end of the decade while reducing Germany’s by up to 0.9%.

“It raises the question, if China’s trade delivers negative value, why trade?” the Danish businessman said.

“If you are suddenly in a situation where trade creates a lot of value for one party, but it destroys value for the other, countries will begin to ask themselves, why do we trade?” he said, pointing to a potential doomsday scenario in which manufacturing disappeared from European countries, causing mass job losses, collapse in the tax base and potential multigenerational unemployment and spiralling social benefits costs.

Speaking in Brussels, where he has been in back-to-back meetings with European Commission officials to give them feedback from Beijing, he said Europe is at an inflection point.

“If we create new dependencies, ultimately that might result in Europe losing its ability to determine its own future,” with mass job losses and fraying of communities, Eskelund added.

That existential threat is already beginning to bite in Europe’s car, chemicals and renewable energy sectors, with the German automotive industry hit by a double whammy of reduced profits in China and cheap competition at home from Chinese companies such as BYD, Geely and SAIC Motor. Last week, BMW said its sales in China had dropped 22% in the first nine months of the year, in line with expectations of suppressed domestic demand, while issuing a second profit warning this year.

Eskelund is not a fan of the term “China shock 2.0” – which refers to the impact of the huge rise in exports of, cars, chemicals and components – on local industry to the EU in the last five years. He said it should not have come as a surprise as Beijing’s plans to displace imports were the enduring theme of the last three five-year plans since Beijing’s Made in China strategy was agreed in 2015.

“I don’t really like the term China shock 2.0 because these things that are happening should not be a shock because it is all written in their five-year plans so you should be able to anticipate that,” Eskelund said.

“The proof of concept here is that China has been laser-focused on reducing its own dependencies over the last 30 years,” he added, pointing to a concomitant reduction in imports.

A recent report by Centre for European Reform (CER) urged Germany to stop “admiring” Beijing and change its strategy, with China’s latest five-year plan (2026 to 2030) a doubling-down on “self-reliance”.

“It is committed to displacing imports in the few sectors where it still depends on foreign supply,” said the CER.

The sectors that have been identified in China’s latest five-year plan are AI, quantum computing; robotics; smart and green tech; biomanufacturing; brain-computer interfaces; and 6G communications.

Solar panels, wind turbines, robots, driverless cars, advanced chips are part of China’s new strategy for global domination.

Eskelund’s advice to those industries in Europe is not to hide but brace themselves for China to deliver. Once a strategy is decided in Beijing, incentives, tax breaksand subsidies flow down by region to town and village levels. Officials across the country know what to focus on and do, he said.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

His advice to EU leaders: “Engage with China and discuss getting to a point where everyone can see value is being created for both sides”, something he thinks Beijing understands.

While this might be an obvious point, Eskelund said a fundamental shift in approach was needed in Europe.

“China has always been very focused on outcomes, whereas Europe has been more focused on principles. It raises the question of whether following principles is delivering undesirable outcomes,” Eskelund said.

A view expressed in some quarters, including the UK, that trade imbalance could be addressed by increasing exports was just “fantasy”, said Eskelund, with this year’s surplus against the rest of the world expected to surpass 2025’s record $1.2tn (£900bn).

Eskelund said many companies were still making good money in China but the brutal competition in the country’s supply chains, combined with a currency undervalued by about 30%, left Europeans in the dust.

“What we see right now is that in almost all industrial verticals, almost anywhere in the world, you would have one and probably several Chinese competitors” he said.

“It’s just very, very difficult in most industries to compete with the Chinese.”

Giving up on trade is not the answer. If you can’t beat them join them. And that is what is now happening, the chamber has found.

“One way that they can compete with the Chinese supply chain is to become a part of the Chinese supply chain itself,” Eskelund said.

“For many companies it’s not the profit that you make in China that matters as much as the way Chinese supply chains enable you to compete on a global marketplace.”

This could mean making the products in China in joint domestic partnerships with European companies and getting into global supply chains in conjunction with Chinese firms.

“It is a sort of evolution from the old days when you came to China to service the Chinese market in China for China. It has now become: be in China, for the world. I think it’s a fairly significant shift,” Eskelund said.

For many of his members this had led to an “uptick in confidence” in the past year.

“This has turned out to be one of the ways that companies adapt, faced with a saturated market in China, they begin to explore exports,” he said.

#116. Author Jojo Moyes among UK travellers at risk of EU ban amid digital border system glitches

Errors in entry and exit records leave some Britons wrongly flagged as having overstayed post-Brexit 90-day limit

EU sues IBM and two European firms over entry/exit border system

The writer Jojo Moyes is facing a two-year EU travel ban and €10,000 (£8,300) fine because of glitches with the bloc’s digital security controls.

She is one of a number of UK travellers who say their trips to the EU have not been properly recorded since the introduction of the EU digital entry and exit system (EES).

The system, which launched last year, has replaced passport stamps with biometrics recorded at border control in Schengen zone countries.

However, the botched rollout has led to some Britons being denied their post-Brexit right to spend 90 days out of 180 in Europe because the system falsely states they have used up their allowance.

Moyes, a bestselling novelist, was detained by French border guards after a holiday and accused of overstaying. She had spent only 23 days in the EU over six months.

She discovered that her departure from Portugal after a trip in May had not been logged on the system.

“The clock had been ticking ever since,” she told the consumer organisation Which? “The border control senior officer at Marseille warned me that I should not attempt to re-enter Europe until it is sorted out, or face a €10,000 fine and a two-year ban.”

Over the summer, some countries suspended the EES and resorted to manual passport checks due to technical glitches and staff shortages. On Monday, it emerged that the EU is suing three firms over the failures.

The problem affects passengers whose biometrics were collected on their arrival in the EU, but not on their departure. In some cases, this means the system has failed to record that they have left.

The rolling 180-day window starts as soon as they enter the EU and, since Brexit, those who are deemed to have remained for more than 90 days face deportation or fines of up to €10,000.

Jane Nolan and her husband discovered that the EES wrongly showed them as having used up their 90-day travel allowance, preventing them from booking a trip to France. “We travelled to France in April by LeShuttle and spent 40 days there,” she said. “We have made no subsequent Schengen visits.

“We should have 50 days remaining, but the EU’s ‘check how long you can stay’ tool says: ‘Authorised stay: not OK,’ and zero remaining days. It does not show us which entry or exit record produced that result.”

The couple’s passports were manually stamped on their return to the UK in June as French authorities had suspended the EES to prevent long queues.

“We have asked the French authorities to check our records, but do not yet know what has gone wrong or whether the result could affect us at the border,” said Nolan.

In a case reported by the Telegraph a holidaymaker from the UK was detained at the Italian border and deported under police guard after a system error suggested she had overstayed in the EU.

Rory Boland, Editor of Which? Travel described the situation as unacceptable.

“This is one of the most serious in a long line of European border control failures,” he said. “The lack of care and accountability shown towards travellers, who risk being locked up and fines of thousands of pounds for rules they have not broken, is utterly indefensible.”

The Advantage Travel Partnership, which represents independent travel agents, said British travellers were being penalised for a chaotic system.

Its CEO, Julia Lo Bue-Said, said: “Travellers should not be penalised for failures in a system that is entirely outside their control. There needs to be greater accountability, alongside a straightforward process to challenge and correct inaccurate records.”

The Foreign Office said the problem was concerning. A spokesperson said: “We are aware of reports of inaccuracies in some EES records affecting British nationals, including incomplete records and the registration of some individuals who are exempt from EES. We are raising these issues as part of our engagement with the European Commission.”

It advised travellers to carry evidence of when they last left the EU and to raise any anomalies with border officials.

Travellers can check how many days of their allowance they have left by typing their passport number into the EES website. This records all visits to the Schengen zone since 10 April 2026.

If there is an unexpected shortfall, they can ask to have their record corrected before travelling by selecting the last country they visited on the Travel to Europe data protection page and contacting the relevant data controller.

Moyes said her efforts to do this have so far been in vain.

“In the last two weeks, I’ve tried every possible avenue,” she said. “I have now engaged an immigration lawyer, but not everyone is lucky enough to be able to afford this remedy. I have no idea how much time they think I have spent in Europe, and no idea whether I am safe to travel.”

The European Commission has been contacted for a comment.

#114. Thirteen migrants die after boat sinks off Crete

Greek authorities said approximately 1,700 migrants reached the island in the first three days of November

At least 13 people have died after a boat carrying migrants sank off the coast of the Greek island of Crete.

The coastguard said it had rescued 18 people, all of whom were Sudanese nationals. Four of the survivors were taken to hospital, including a toddler.

Authorities said it was not immediately clear what caused the deaths, but they believe the boat had been at sea for days and had struggled to reach shore due to strong winds.

Crete remains one of several key gateways for migrants attempting to reach Europe from the Middle East and North Africa, with more than 22,000 of the 86,400 sea arrivals so far this year landing in Crete alone.

One unnamed coastguard official who spoke to news agency Reuters on condition of anonymity said the survivors were "shivering from the cold".

The migrants' boat was spotted by a Moroccan-flagged container ship about 15 nautical miles (28km) off Crete's coast.

The Greek coastguard rescued another 187 people in three separate incidents off the coast of Greece on Monday.

More than 37,000 migrants have arrived in Greece so far this year, UN figures show, already surpassing the number who arrived in the entirety of 2025.

The Greek government has announced plans to deal with the influx, including opening a temporary housing complex near the Cretan capital, Heraklion.

Greece was at the heart of Europe's migration crisis in 2015-16, when nearly a million people arrived on its islands, mostly from Turkey.

Arrivals later fell as border controls tightened, but crossings from Libya have surged in recent years, particularly among people fleeing conflict in Sudan and seeking better economic opportunities from countries including Bangladesh.

#111. Viktor Orbán’s brother detained in people-smuggling investigation, Hungarian PM says

Arrest made by anti-corruption body set up to investigate alleged graft during former PM’s 16-year rule

Hungarian authorities have detained a brother of Viktor Orbán as part of an investigation into people-smuggling, the prime minister, Péter Magyar, has said.

The Hungarian anti-graft body, the national asset protection and recovery office, said in ⁠a statement that a joint operation with police had led to the arrests of four people and searches of homes in Budapest and Pest county.

While the statement did not name the three men and one woman who had been detained, Magyar linked one of the arrests to the former prime minister.

“Three ex-ministers of Orbán are already in custody,” Magyar said in Berlin, where he gave a speech on Monday evening. “Already his brother today ⁠went to custody,” he added, referring to the former leader’s sibling Aron Orbán, 49.

In April, Magyar won a landslide election victory, ousting Orbán after 16 years in power during which the country slumped in rankings to become the most corrupt in the EU. In the leadup to the vote, Magyar crisscrossed Hungary vowing to investigate any allegations of corruption and abuses of office that had taken place during his rival’s rule.

On Monday as news broke of his brother’s arrest, Viktor Orbán accused officials of carrying out a “political witch-hunt”, in a post on social media. “The aim of this dirty political game is to eliminate opponents and establish a one-party state. I am the real target. My younger brother is simply being used to get to me,” said Orbán, who remains president of his rightwing nationalist Fidesz party.

Shortly after taking power in May, Magyar set up the national asset protection and recovery office, an independent body with a mandate to uncover any past abuses of public funds and prevent future violations. He described the office as a central plank of a wider anti-graft campaign that he called “Operation Purgatory”.

Magyar has alleged that corruption, including the misuse of public funds, may have cost Hungarians as much as 10% of the country’s gross domestic product in recent years. Orbán has denied all allegations of wrongdoing.

Police said in a statement on Monday that the four people detained were suspected of helping Vietnamese citizens reach western Europe for several months in 2024. The timeline suggests the alleged activities took place while Orbán was still in office.

The perpetrators were accused of assisting more than 100 Vietnamese citizens in obtaining visas at Hungary’s foreign missions in Vietnam, in exchange for a ⁠ recruitment fee which they then shared, the authorities said.

The four detained are suspected “of human smuggling committed as part of a criminal conspiracy for the purpose of financial ‌gain”, police added.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

One of the suspects is alleged to have engaged in influence peddling during the process of acquiring visas.

The arrest is the latest in a series of high-profile police actions against former officials or other individuals linked to the former prime minister.

In the months since Orbán lost power, three former ministers from his government have been taken into custody over graft accusations in separate cases, including the former central bank governor György Matolcsy. All have denied any wrongdoing.

#110. Krakow shooting suspect has died in hospital, police say

A gunman suspected of killing six people in central Krakow, as well as his parents and sister at their home, died in hospital early on Monday, Polish police have said.

The 24-year-old, who has been named as Michal Oksinski by local media, was injured after opening fire in central Krakow, the second largest city in Poland, on Saturday, police said.

Armed officers raced to the scene and engaged in a gunfight with the suspect, who was shot within three minutes of the attack beginning, preventing a further massacre, officials said.

Authorities initially reported that the gunman had apparently been inspired by "far-right ideologies", but have since said they are investigating any possible motivation.

After the shooting, which began shortly before 15:00 local time (14:00 GMT) in Krakow's busy Main Market Square, police identified the attacker as a 24-year-old Polish man.

In the hours after the shooting, a minister said that early investigations indicate he was inspired by "far-right ideologies". The minister also described him as an "ultra-nationalist" without giving further details.

However, during a press briefing on Monday morning, PrzemysÅaw Nowak, a spokesperson for Poland's National Prosecutor's Office, said investigations into his motive remain ongoing.

Six people - four women and two men - are confirmed to have been killed in the attack.

People run for shelter as gunshots heard in Krakow

Four victims are Polish, while a British national and an Austrian woman were also killed.

Poland's Prime Minister Donald Tusk said three bodies - two women and one man - were found at the gunman's house when it was searched after the attack.

On Sunday, Polish police confirmed those three victims had been identified as the alleged gunman's 53-year-old father, 52-year-old mother and 21-year-old sister.

Polish media has reported the suspect had a firearms permit and may have obtained the weapons from his family home.

Four firearms were found in the gunman's car, including two AR-15 rifles and two handguns, along with several hundred rounds of ammunition, Nowak said. Authorities also seized at least four phones from his car and person, and electronic equipment from his home.

Sixteen people, including five foreigners, two police officers and the attacker, were taken to hospital. The interior ministry later said they were from Austria, Britain, Germany, Portugal and Russia.

Speaking on Sunday, one of two surgeons who treated victims on the street after the attack said his colleague "undoubtedly" saved the life of a gravely injured police officer.

The two surgeons, who were eating near the Main Market Square when the gunman opened fire, rushed to the scene to treat both the attacker and the two injured police officers.

Watch: Doctor who treated victims of Krakow shooting speaks to the BBC

Professor Michal PÄdziwiatr told the BBC: "We saw the shooting attack. And what a surgeon can do in a situation like this?... We decided to help the victims as soon as possible."

"We rushed into the area where the shooting was and started our rescue, because there were no ambulances available, there were no professionals around."

PÄdziwiatr said his colleague, who has been named by local media as Dr Mateusz Wierdak, used his hand to stem the bleeding from the most severely injured policeman, allowing time for an ambulance to arrive.

On Sunday it was reported that both injured officers had emerged from comas.

Witnesses who were near the Main Market Square have described their panic and confusion as the attack unfolded.

Ruby, from London, heard gunfire and "yelled at my friends to run and we all sprinted for our lives".

She said they "just kept sprinting" until they found a hotel to shelter inside.

Tom Brook, from West Yorkshire, was inside a McDonald's near the square when he heard "multiple gunshots and saw people running for cover".

"I then walked into the square and saw paramedics working on someone outside [a shop] and there was a dead body covered in foil blankets 20 metres away," he said.

In the wake of the shooting, Poland declared the country would enter two days of national mourning.

After travelling to Krakow, Tusk said: "Until now in Poland we haven't had such drastic cases of terrorism...that's what we have to call this cruel attack on people's lives here in Krakow."

UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham said he was "shocked and saddened by the appalling shooting in KrakÃ³w".

"My thoughts are with the families of those who have lost their lives, everyone injured and all those affected by this senseless violence. The United Kingdom stands in solidarity with the people of Poland," he added.

Main Market Square reopened on Sunday evening after a security cordon was lifted.

#108. US officials seek ‘final end’ to Russia-Ukraine war as Abu Dhabi talks begin

First substantive peace talks since February to include US proposal for ‘long-term security architecture’ between Moscow and Europe

US officials have said they are seeking a “final end” to the war between Russia and Ukraine, beginning with trilateral talks in Abu Dhabi on Tuesday, the first substantive negotiations between the two countries since February.

The talks are the first since the US launched joint strikes with Israel against Iran, and they follow bruising midterm elections that have weakened Donald Trump at home. The first day of talks ended early Tuesday afternoon local time with a second day set for Wednesday.

Part of the Trump administration’s proposal involves drawing up a new “long-term security architecture” between Russia and Europe, administration officials have said.

European officials are concerned that Russia may stick to its hardline demands that Nato should roll back its military footprint across eastern Europe, which would threaten to leave allies vulnerable to Moscow’s influence at a time of historic tensions with the west.

Before the trilateral talks, European officials held separate discussions on Tuesday with representatives from Ukraine and the US, where they agreed “any future arrangements must respect sovereignty, territorial integrity, independent decision-making, and provide credible security guarantees for Ukraine”, according to a joint statement.

European leaders are not convinced Russia intends to negotiate in good faith. “So far they have just been pretending that they negotiate because we haven’t seen any ⁠goodwill from the Russian side,” the EU high representative for foreign policy, Kaja Kallas, told Reuters on Monday.

European countries want to be involved in discussions, although there are signs of unease about the proliferating strands of peace talks. Italy’s prime minister, Giorgia Meloni, last week complained about the “disorderly” process of meetings and phone calls that she argued weakened Europe’s ability to speak with one voice.

Since his first decade in power, Vladimir Putin has complained about Nato’s eastward expansion and sought to renegotiate the balance of power between Nato and Russia – but never before have those demands been embraced by a sitting US administration, which has made it clear that it is desperate to get to a deal.

Previewing the discussions, a US administration official said the US would “need Europe at the table” to end the war. He said negotiators could also revive the Nato-Russian council that was suspended after Russia’s full-scale invasion in 2022, and potentially lift sanctions against Russia to get to a deal.

The US officials also claimed that they had discussed with Russia potential security guarantees to Ukraine similar to Nato’s article 5, which protects member states in case of an attack. Russia has strongly opposed Nato admitting Ukraine or western countries placing troops in Ukraine.

“What they would be getting would be effectively very similar to Nato,” an administration official said.

“Getting a US security guarantee is a really, really big deal,” the official added, saying it would be up to Donald Trump and Ukraine’s Volodymyr Zelenskyy to discuss. “And if he wants to do that, that’s that’s a very, very big deal in terms of resources and funding and military support.”

Critics have expressed scepticism about what may trigger those security guarantees, however, noting that article 5 does not oblige Nato member states to defend others, and that cyber-attacks and other forms of hybrid warfare have blurred the line for what would require a military response.

Jared Kushner, Trump’s son-in-law and a lead negotiator for the US, described a potential new framework for Europe in general terms on Monday, saying the US would seek “stronger consultation, reliable crisis communications, greater military transparency, and reciprocal measures to reduce escalation risks, while fully respecting the sovereignty and independent decision-making of every nation”.

European delegations from Germany, France, the UK, Italy and the EU will meet US negotiators in Abu Dhabi to discuss the “long-term security architecture between Russia and Europe in order to figure out how to reduce future conflicts”.

A European security official from a country attending the talks said that the US proposal was not yet clear and that they were bracing themselves for the US to pose serious concessions to Russia during the Abu Dhabi consultations.

“We already have a European security architecture in place,” said Kallas at a G7 foreign ministers meeting on Monday. “This includes international agreements under which Russia has undertaken obligations, including with regard to sovereignty and territorial integrity. The problem is that Russia is not respecting these agreements.”

Donald Trump has also said that Ukraine would probably be expected to give up territory in a final deal, and would end with a “little bit different shape”.

After meeting the Kremlin adviser Kirill Dmitriev last week, Trump said he would seek a “big, beautiful agreement” that “might involve all of Europe”. Critics are concerned that Trump and Putin could try to go over the heads of European leaders in order to negotiate a peace that gives serious concessions to Russia.

“There’s a lot of baggage that people in Europe have about this idea,” said a US administration official, pointing specifically to Russia’s 2021 proposal that was viewed as an ultimatum to Nato to get out of eastern Europe.

At that time, Russia issued a list of demands to Nato that included a ban on expansion to new countries including Ukraine, a rollback of troops to its pre-1997 borders, and limits on where it could place missiles. Nato rejected that proposal, and Russia invaded Ukraine less than a month later.

The US administration official said: “We have to build something new that works today … Because if all the old architecture was working well, then we wouldn’t be in this long war. And so obviously something’s not working, and we need to come up with a way that when we end this war, it really is a final end to the war.”

The content of those proposals are not clear yet, although the secretary of state, Marco Rubio, claimed on Monday “there’s been some advances made in what that architecture should look”.

“We obviously have to get past the conflict to get to that point,” he added.

#107. At least 39 die after Europe-bound migrant boat sinks off Senegal

Spanish migrant rights group Walking Borders estimates that for every 100 people who reach the Canary Islands by sea, 21 die during the crossing (file photo)

At least 39 people have died after a boat carrying migrants sank off the coast of Senegal, the Senegalese Press Agency (APS) reports.

The interior ministry said one person had been rescued, but the number of people still missing was yet to be established.

The small wooden boat capsized between the capital, Dakar and the northern city of Saint-Louis, as it headed towards Spain's Canary Islands.

Spanish migrant rights group Walking Borders recorded 635 deaths on the Atlantic route to the Canary Islands between January and May 2026, the highest toll among the routes it monitors.

Saliou Diouf, founder of Senegalese migrant aid group Boza Fii, told the AFP news agency that all the bodies recovered by Sunday evening were Senegalese. The nationalities of other people on board was yet to be established, as search operations continued.

Four bodies were recovered on Monday morning in an advanced state of decomposition and buried following a collective funeral prayer organised by the fire service.

Rescue teams are searching along a 25km (15-mile)- stretch of coastline, but operations have been complicated by difficult access and strong ocean currents, which are pushing bodies further south.

Authorities say 24 of the 39 victims have been identified and their bodies returned to their families, while 11 others are still awaiting identification.

President Bassirou Diomaye Faye has expressed his condolences to the victims' families and urged Senegalese nationals not to risk their lives on the dangerous sea crossings.

In a statement posted on X, he pledged to intensify efforts to dismantle the networks organising the journeys.

"We will relentlessly continue the fight against those who organise these crossings and the work that gives our young people reasons to hope here," he said.

The tragedy comes amid renewed concerns over the dangers facing migrant attempting to reach Europe by sea.

Walking Borders estimates that about 21 people died for every 100 who reached the Canary Islands during the period it monitored.

Senegalese authorities said they had intercepted another boat, on Friday, carrying 142 migrants attempting to reach Spain.

Those on board included 23 women, two babies and two other children. Police said the group comprised nationals of Senegal, Gambia, Guinea-Bissau, Guinea and Nigeria.

Meanwhile, a state of emergency was declared on the Spanish island of El Hierro on Saturday following a surge in migrant arrivals.

More than 3,300 people have reached the island in about 30 boats since the beginning of November, according to local authorities.

This amounts to almost a third of the population on the island, one of the main entry points into Europe for migrants arriving from West Africa.

Fernando Clavijo, president of the Canary Islands regional government, described the situation as "absolutely unsustainable," reports the Reuters news agency.

The route has grown in prominence amid tighter controls and increased cooperation between European countries and North African states, which may have diverted some journeys away from the central Mediterranean.

Limited employment opportunities, family pressures and established migration networks are among the factors driving people to leave Senegal, according to the International Organization for Migration (IOM).

For many young people, the prospect of finding work and earning more abroad remains a powerful incentive, despite the risks of crossing the Atlantic in overcrowded boat.

Nearly one in four young people in Senegal was unemployed in the second quarter of 2026, according to the country's statistics agency.

The figure highlights the challenge facing President Faye's government as it seeks to create jobs for a predominantly young population that overwhelmingly voted for him in the 2024 elections.

In a statement on Sunday, Senegal's interior ministry said it would continue efforts to combat people-smuggling networks, including by strengthening cooperation with neighbouring countries.

#105. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#103. Trump questions midterm elections integrity after previously accepting results

Trump has previously cast doubt on the integrity of US election results

US President Donald Trump has questioned the legitimacy of the midterm election results in which Republicans suffered sweeping losses, days after saying he would accept the outcome.

"Does anybody really believe that this 'Election' was honest. How could the Polls be so wrong?" Trump wrote on Truth Social on Monday.

This appeared to contradict what he told the New York Times on election night that he was "absolutely willing" to accept the results.

Democrats retook both chambers of Congress in last week's elections. State and local officials across the US said voting had been mostly problem-free.

Some key races, including Senate contests in Alaska and Mississippi, have yet to be called.

Trump says US voter fraud is 'rampant' - evidence suggests it is extremely rare

In the Truth Social post, Trump made vague claims about polling, voting "dumps", and "machines" without providing specific evidence of what this referred to and whether he believed there had been error or wrongdoing.

The reporting of vote counts on election night proceeded as normal, according to Charles Stewart III, a political scientist at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT).

"We saw across the nation the standard patterns we see in terms of when the votes are reported," he said.

Stewart added that in some states, like Florida and Georgia, some initial reporting of results happens quickly because they already have the results of early and mail ballots.

Trump has previously attempted to cast doubts on the integrity of elections he has lost, although he himself was not on the ballot in the midterms.

"We would have won easily if I was on the ballot," Trump told CBS News, the BBC's US partner, after it was confirmed his party had lost both the Senate and the House.

Surveys ahead of the election from several pollsters and news outlets suggested that Republicans could lose seats in both the House and Senate.

As it stands, Democrats have won 54 US Senate seats and 254 US House seats in the midterms, giving them comfortable majorities in both chambers of Congress even as counting continues in some races, like in Alaska and Mississippi.

"State laws and practicalities are such that some states will just take longer to count," said Ruth Greenwood, director of the Election Law Clinic at Harvard Law School.

"That doesn't mean anything nefarious is occurringâwe just need to trust local election officials to count every vote and tell us the results when they are done."

The outcome has been clear since election night. Trump told the New York Times then: "We'll, you know, look forward to working with [Democrats]", adding: "We'll probably work something out."

He has also offered his own analysis of why Republicans lost, acknowledging on Fox News that the Iran war had contributed to the defeat.

"People agree with what I'm doing on Iran but they really probably weren't willing to do the short term consequences of paying more for gasoline - I understand that," he said.

Vice-President JD Vance has also blamed the Iran war, which began in February and which has severely disrupted global oil and gas supplies - causing costs to soar, as the reason their party lost the election.

In the run-up to the midterms, Trump made claims about widespread voting fraud, despite evidence that it is rare.

He and his allies alleged for months that the 2020 presidential election, which he lost to former President Joe Biden, had been rigged against him. His lawyers and allies spent weeks attempting to litigate the results, but courts repeatedly struck them down.

The justice department during his administration has made unprecedented attempts to exert more federal control over elections - a power the Constitution primarily delegates to individual states.

Days before these midterms, it filed controversial lawsuits in federal courts challenging the count and certification of votes in states like California and Alaska, but judges ruled against them in nearly all instances.

Election law experts, state officials, and local elections offices told the BBC they had spent months preparing for a scenario in which the White House or election sceptics questioned the votes' legitimacy.

Despite the intense focus around election integrity on both sides of the debate, top state and local election officials have reported a mostly smooth process so far for the 2026 midterms.

Extreme weather did force some location closures, and a handful of reported administrative or technical issues slowed the process in some local jurisdictions.

Supreme Court rejects Trump's request to stop California counting disputed votes

#102. Russia-Ukraine talks ‘very productive,’ says US’s Rubio – Europe live

US-led talks in Abu Dhabi break up after four hours but are expected to resume tomorrow

US officials seek ‘final end’ to Russia-Ukraine war as talks begin

Speaking after Russia, Ukraine and the US held trilateral talks today in the UAE, US secretary of state Marco Rubio has said the talks were “very productive”, but that there was still a lot of work to be done.

Reuters reports Rubio also said that it was a goal at some point to expand the trilateral talks to include other European voices.

Europe’s manufacturers could give up on attempting to compete with their Chinese counterparts by the end of the decade if current trends continue amid fears over mass job losses, the head of the European Union Chamber of Commerce in China has warned.

In a case of “if you can’t beat them, join them”, Jens Eskelund said European companies operating in China were now thinking “Made in China for China” could be replaced by a philosophy of “Made in China for the world”.

Eskelund, who heads the influential Beijing-headquartered body, said European manufacturers could find themselves at an existential crossroads by the end of the decade, asking themselves: “What is the point of trading?”

As the EU gets tough with China with a deficit running at a record £1bn (€1.18bn) a day and an increasing mood for protectionism in Brussels, Berlin and Paris, he said European countries might need to shift their policies to allow domestic manufacturers to become part of Chinese firms’ supply chains, rather than treat them as competitors.

Speaking after Russia, Ukraine and the US held trilateral talks today in the UAE, US secretary of state Marco Rubio has said the talks were “very productive”, but that there was still a lot of work to be done.

Reuters reports Rubio also said that it was a goal at some point to expand the trilateral talks to include other European voices.

Iran has responded to the accusation by France that it was responsible for the recent cyber-attacks on supermarket fridges in the European country, declaring them “baseless”.

AFP reports that in a statement, Tehran said “Iran’s foreign ministry categorically rejects the French government’s baseless and one-sided accusations against Iran regarding alleged cyber activities in France”, adding that it “strongly condemns this hackneyed policy of levelling accusations against the Islamic republic”.

The first day of talks between Russia, Ukraine and the US in the UAE have ended after four hours.

The press office of the Foreign Intelligence Service of Ukraine has stated they are expected to resume tomorrow. The Ukrainian delegation has also spoken to representatives from Nato and Europe as part of their mission.

We reported in an earlier post that ten people had been killed after dozens of vehicles were involved in a traffic accident on the Trakia motorway in south-eastern Bulgaria.

In an update, Bulgaria’s health minister, Katya Ivkova, said 10 people died at the scene and two others died while being transported to hospital, bringing the total deaths to 12. She said 46 other people were transported to medical facilities for treatment.

The EU’s climate chief, Wopke Hoekstra, has admitted that expectations were “low” from the Cop31 summit in Turkey this year, but said there was hope for “meaningful” progress on clean electricity.

The summit has entered its second day with many world leaders trying to forge agreements that stop climate breakdown even if the political backdrop makes progress more difficult than it needs to be. You can follow the latest in our Cop31 blog here:

The EU has said it stands ready “to come with a swift and united response” after France blamed Iran for a spate of cyber-attacks on supermarket freezers and chillers that caused millions of euros of damage.

Speaking to journalists on Monday, French foreign minister Jean-Noël Barrot said “Europe-wide sanctions” would be imposed against those responsible as he announced France believed that Iran’s Revolutionary Guards were “responsible for this attack”.

Speaking on Wednesday, a European Commission spokesperson acknowledged the French attribution, without endorsing it. The spokesperson said:

double quotation markWe acknowledge that France has attributed these (cyber-attacks) to Iran. Pending discussions with member states, we as (the) European Union, we stand ready to come with a swift and united response.

We as the EU, we have consistently called on all states to refrain from malicious behaviour in the cyberspace. And we will continue to address such malicious behavior, making use of all the tools that we have also under the EU cyber diplomacy toolbox, including sanctions.

In a major decision earlier this year, the EU designated Iran’s IRGC a terrorist organisation, ending years of division over the issue. The designation became possible after France dropped its long-held opposition, having previously feared it would damage diplomatic channels and the interests of French nationals in Iran.

Similar incidents have hit retailers in a number European countries including Belgium, Estonia, Ireland, Finland, Luxembourg, the Netherlands and Sweden.

A spokesperson for Belgium’s foreign ministry said they took the information provided by France “very seriously”. An investigation into incidents that took place in early November in Belgium was still ongoing, the spokesperson said, adding: “we have no evidence that would allow us to attribute these incidents to Iran or any other actor, nor can we establish a link with the earlier attacks in France”.

Belgium has “repeatedly condemned Iranian cyber-attacks and other hostile activities” and was open to examining the French proposal for sanctions, “based on the available evidence,” the person added.

In other news, Portugal will shut schools and non-esesential public services on Wednesday, with Storm Charles expected to bring torrential rain and heavy winds to parts of the country.

“All those who can should stay at home,” the country’s interior minister Luis Neves told a press conference on Tuesday, warning people to stay away from coastal areas and to work from home where possible.

“It’s a tough, difficult time, and the country will be put to the test, especially tomorrow, given what has been presented as the scenario,” he was quoted by the Portugese news outlet Diario de Noticias as having told journalists. He said a state of alert will be in effect from between 00:00 and 23:59 on Wednesday.

Eight of the country’s districts, including the capital Lisbon, are on the highest red alert for extreme weather, with the storm expected to reach mainland Portugal on Wednesday morning after passing the island of Madeira this evening.

An official from the Portugal’s environment agency said 101 municipalities are at risk of flooding, noting the peak of the tide is expected to be recorded tomorrow at 3:20pm local time.

Mainland Portugal is still under the influence of Storm Bruna, centred north of the Azores, which has brought heavy rain and caused travel disruption.

Hungarian authorities have detained the brother of Viktor Orbán as part of a people-smuggling ⁠investigation, the prime minister, Péter Magyar, has said.

The Hungarian anti-graft body, the national asset protection and recovery office (NVVH), said in ⁠a statement it and ⁠police had ​detained four people and searched homes in Budapest and Pest county.

“Three ex-ministers of Orbán are already in custody,” Magyar said in Berlin, where he was giving a speech. “Already his brother today ⁠went to custody,” he added, referring to the former prime minister’s sibling, Aron, ​49.

Writing on Facebook, Orbán dismissed ‌his brother’s detention as a case of political revenge. “In this dirty game, the aim is the elimination of ‌the political opponent and the establishment of a one-party system. I am the target, my younger brother is just a tool,” the former prime minister wrote.

The NVVH ‌was set up by Magyar to investigate allegations of corruption during Orbán’s 16-year rule. The latter has denied the claims.

Magyar ​ousted Orbán in an April election and the NVVH is central to his government’s anti-graft campaign which he has dubbed “Operation Purgatory”. You can read more here:

The substations ‌that were hit ​were in the ⁠Poltava, Dnipropetrovsk, Vinnytsia ​and Kirovohrad ​regions, ​Koretskyi ​said. Russian attacks killed at least nine people and injured 39 others across Ukraine over the past day, according to a report in the Kyiv Independent.

The attacks came after Volodymyr Zelenskyy, who has urged the US to increase its flagging aid to Ukraine, accused Russia of intensifying strikes across the country to put pressure on Kyiv at the Abu Dhabi talks (see this post for more details).

A police officer from Spain’s canine unit was killed ⁠and an expert from the explosives disposal unit was seriously injured in ⁠a backpack explosion in the northern Spanish city of Oviedo, according to authorities.

The officers had been deployed to an address in the Villafría neighbourhood after a previous alert about a shooting in which a 17-year-old girl was injured, according to the Spanish outlet El Mundo.

When the officers arrived, they saw a partially opened backpack with a flashlight, prompting them to call the bomb squad (Tedax) for assistance, El Mundo reports.

An officer from the canine unit approached the bag to inspect it, but it reportedly then exploded, killing him, and injuring the bomb disposal expert that was with him. The US special envoy Steve Witkoff, who is leading the American delegation alongside Jared Kushner - Donald Trump’s son-in-law – has just posted on X with details of their meeting with European and Ukrainian officials ahead of the trilateral talks between the US, Ukraine and Russia.

Witkoff said the meeting helped to coordinate their efforts to end the war and “establish the conditions for a durable peace”, including support for “robust security guarantees” for Kyiv. He said officials from Europe, the US and Ukraine shared the same objectives and all held a commitment to “Euro-Atlantic security”.

double quotation markParticipants emphasized that any future arrangements must respect sovereignty, territorial integrity, independent decision-making, and provide credible security guarantees for Ukraine.

The shared objective is to develop a practical and mutually reinforcing security framework that strengthens Ukraine’s long-term security and builds the confidence necessary to reach a negotiated settlement.

“Participants further recognized that addressing postwar security concerns and reducing the risk of conflict are essential. They discussed how to ensure strong mechanisms for managing risk and avoiding dangerous escalation,” Witkoff added.

Witkoff, a property developer and a longtime friend of Donald Trump, took a leading role in Ukraine-Russia negotiations with little diplomatic experience, effectively doing the role usually reserved for the secretary of state.

Donald Trump has said that Ukraine would like be expected to give up territory in a final deal, and would end with a “little bit different shape“.

After meeting with Kremlin advisor Kirill Dmitriev last week, Trump said he would seek a “big, beautiful agreement” that “might involve all of Europe”. Critics are concerned that Trump and Putin could try to go over the heads of European leaders in order to negotiate a peace that gives serious concessions to Russia.

“There’s a lot of baggage that people in Europe have about this idea,” said a US administration official, pointing specifically to Russia’s 2021 proposal that was viewed as an ultimatum to Nato to get out of eastern Europe.

In 2021, Russia issued a list of demands to Nato that included a ban on expansion to new countries including Ukraine, a rollback of troops to its pre-1997 borders, and limits on where it could place missiles. Nato rejected that proposal and Russia invaded Ukraine less than a month later.

“We have to build something new that works today… Because if all the old architecture was working well, then we wouldn’t be in this long war. And so obviously something’s not working, and we need to come up with a way that when we end this war, it really is a final end to the war,” said the US administration official.

The content of those proposals are not clear yet, although the secretary of state, Marco Rubio, claimed on Monday “there’s been some advances made in what that architecture should look”. “We obviously have to get past the conflict to get to that point,” he added.

We are restarting our Europe live blog ahead of the US-led trilateral talks in Abu Dhabi that seek an end to the war between Russia and Ukraine.

The Ukrainian president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, said on the eve of the talks that Ukrainian battlefield successes were strengthening Kyiv’s position in negotiations.

The ⁠three-sided talks between Ukrainian, Russian and ⁠US negotiators ​are to open on Tuesday and will be the first substantive talks since February aimed at ending the war, which has raged on for more than four-and-a-half years.

“Ukrainian forces are currently genuinely strengthening Ukraine’s negotiating position,” Zelenskyy said in his nightly video address on Monday. “Every task that is fully accomplished – every defensive operation that ⁠increases Ukraine’s strength and delivers results – strengthens Ukraine and, above all, strengthens us in negotiations.”

US envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner (who is also Donald Trump’s son-in-law) are to lead the American delegation at the talks, which are scheduled for two ⁠days. In Washington, ⁠Trump said on Monday that the US was seeing ​a “very strong inclination” towards ending Russia’s war, and that both sides were “tired of fighting”.

A US official said security guarantees for Ukraine would be a central topic for the discussions, including the potential for Kyiv to be granted a Nato-style article 5 defence, which protects member states in case of an attack. Any territorial agreement would probably come at the end of negotiations once other items were agreed upon, including a postwar security architecture, the official said.

But Ukraine and Russia have both been adamant about territory, with neither side willing to make any major concessions during previous rounds of diplomacy. Ukraine wants Russia pushed back to the original border between the two countries, while the Russian president, Vladimir Putin, wants to obtain all of eastern Ukraine’s Donbas region.

European officials, ⁠including from France, Germany and the European Commission, ⁠will not directly take part in negotiations, as per Russia’s insistence. But instead the Ukrainian delegation, led by foreign intelligence chief Rustem Umerov, will meet separately to brief their European allies on progress.

The two US envoys met Ukrainian and European officials in Abu Dhabi ahead of the trilateral talks this morning. “Our key priorities are ending the fighting, securing reliable postwar security guarantees, ensuring Ukraine’s economic recovery and development, and taking humanitarian steps,” Umerov wrote in a social media post.

European officials, who have been largely excluded from peace talks, wanted to be involved directly in the talks amid concerns the US administration could try to force through a deal favourable to Russia. #101. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#99. Nine Zimbabweans caught allegedly trying to leave South Africa to fight in Ukraine

Hundreds of African citizens have been recruited to join the Russia-Ukraine war in recent years (file photo)

Nine Zimbabweans have been arrested in South Africa after they were caught allegedly trying to leave the country to fight in the war between Russia and the Ukraine, police say.

The group, aged between 20 and 26, were arrested in and around Johannesburg by a multidisciplinary team on Sunday evening.

They were caught as they were "preparing to board flights out of the country", authorities added, but their final destination is unclear.

They have been charged with contravening South Africa's Regulation of Foreign Military Assistance Act, which outlaws mercenary activity without government permission.

Police said five people were arrested at South Africa's biggest airport, OR Tambo, and another four at a hotel in Bruma, east of Johannesburg by a multidisciplinary team that involved Interpol.

All nine later appeared in court, according to state prosecutors.

The police statement added that the involvement of Interpol and other international law enforcement agencies indicated that the suspects had also allegedly violated laws in their respective home countries, prompting cooperation with South African authorities.

They have also not said whether the group allegedly intended to fight for Russia or Ukraine nor provided details of the organisations allegedly involved in facilitating their travel.

Citizens of several African countries, including Kenya, Ghana and South Africa, have gone to fight for Russia in Ukraine - some they had been duped and had not realised where they were going.

A Ukrainian intelligence assessment estimated earlier this year that more than 1,700 people from 36 countries in Africa have been recruited to fight for Russia.

Kenya at the time estimated that 252 citizens had been illegally conscripted in Russia. In March, Foreign Minister Musalia Mudavadi visited Moscow to address the issue.

He said at the time that an agreement had been reached to stop recruiting Kenyan nationals.

In March, Zimbabwe said that 15 of its nationals had been killed after being recruited to fight for Russia in Ukraine, with more than 60 still trapped on the frontlines.

The previous month, South Africa secured the return of 17 South Africans who were allegedly tricked into joining the war.

In December last year, South African authorities arrested five people, including a journalist, for allegedly recruiting people to join the war.

Ukraine has also been recruiting foreign nationals, including from Africa.
