#97. Mortgage holders’ confidence crashes to lowest level since 1994 as year that ‘feels like a recession’ looms

Westpac survey results signal growing sense of ‘helplessness’ while Jim Chalmers faces growing pressure to rein in spending

Confidence among mortgaged households has crashed to its lowest level in more than three decades amid a growing sense of “helplessness” about the economy, even as a dramatic shift in financial markets suggests there is now a strong chance of a Reserve Bank rate cut by this time next year.

The latest Westpac survey showed its consumer sentiment index sinking again in November to reach a four-year low, amid deepening pessimism about the near-term prospects for the economy.

As Labor comes under increasing pressure over persistent cost-of-living pressures, the prime minister, Anthony Albanese, played down tensions with the treasurer, Jim Chalmers.

A day after the frontbencher Murray Watt called for colleagues to stop undermining Chalmers, Albanese said: “The treasurer is doing a great job”.

The PM said he was working closely with Chalmers in the lead-up to next month’s mid-year budget update, which will include cuts to government spending as pressure mounts to contain inflation.

“This is a treasurer who’s produced a couple of budget surpluses, lower deficits, [who is] producing economic reform,” Albanese said.

Matthew Hassan, Westpac’s head of economic forecasting, said the mood among households was last this bad in 2022, when inflation was running close to 8% and after the RBA had lifted borrowing costs by 4 percentage points over the previous year.

“Prior to that, readings this weak had only ever been seen in the economy’s darkest moments: at the onset of the Covid pandemic; the height of the GFC; and during the recessions in the early 1990s and early 1980s,” Hassan said.

Hassan said while the economic backdrop was not as extreme now as it was during the pandemic and its immediate aftermath, there were “shades of Covid in the responses” from households.

Confidence in the economic outlook over the coming year reached its weakest since August 2020, the survey showed, matching a low point last recorded during the pandemic.

“The comparison with August 2020 is quite useful as that was when Melbourne went into its second lockdown and just before the government massively scaled up income support,” Hassan said.

“There was a sense of helplessness at that stage; there was nothing we could do to avoid the pandemic, and there was nothing the government could do [to avoid the pandemic]. Obviously when the jobkeeper measures were put in place that comforted a lot of people.

“There is a similar feeling of helplessness now. You can argue a substantial portion of what is happening is out of our control, such as rising fuel prices, and there’s only so much the authorities can do to cushion Australians from that impact.”

Indebted households were gloomier still, the survey showed, with pessimism among this group at its worst in records stretching back to 1994.

The latest survey comes a week after Michele Bullock, the RBA governor, warned that the year ahead would “feel like it’s a recession” thanks to persistent cost-of-living pressures alongside weak economic growth.

After five rate increases this year, nearly three in four respondents said they expected another rate hike over the coming 12 months.

Even as the mood in the community darkens, financial markets have begun factoring in the chance of lower interest rates from the middle of next year.

The implied probability of an RBA rate cut was more than 50% by August next year, according to ANZ data, and a decrease to 4.6% was fully priced in by December 2027.

Investors have been rethinking their forecasts for central bank moves alongside a rapid retreat in the global oil price.

After testing a post-Iran war high of nearly US$118 a barrel at the end of October, the global benchmark, Brent crude, has plunged by 20% to below US$94.

“Our base case is that rates do start to come down from August next year, and we don’t see any further hikes,” Hassan said.

#96. Trial date of alleged Charlie Kirk killer set for June 2028

Kirk was a prominent conservative youth activist

The trial of Tyler Robinson, the man accused of killing conservative activist Charlie Kirk, will start in June 2028, a judge has ordered.

Robinson, 23, has pleaded not guilty to aggravated murder and six other charges in connection with the September 2025 killing of Kirk, 31, who was shot while he spoke at a Utah college campus.

Utah County judge Tony Graf Jr on Monday told the court that jury selection would begin on 21 June, 2028. The prosecution has estimated the trial could last 17 weeks, while defence lawyers have said it could take seven months.

Robinson could face the death penalty if found guilty of aggravated murder, something his defence team has sought to prevent.

Prosecutors previously told the court they could be ready for trial by October 2027 but were willing to move that date back to January 2028 to avoid the holiday period.

But defence attorneys said they needed more time to prepare their case and floated a 2029 start. As well as trying to stop Robinson facing the death penalty, they have also tried to have cameras banned from the courtroom.

Kirk's widow, Erika, with whom he had two children, had pushed for as early a trial date as possible while also arguing proceedings should be live-streamed. She, along with Kirk's parents, â Robert and Kathryn, have attended a number of court hearings in the case.

Robinson's legal team is trying to get the death penalty taken off the table in his trial

At the end of Monday's proceedings, Erica Kirk's lawyer, Jeffrey A Nieman, told the judge that while "a trial in the summer of 2028, three years after Charlie's assassination, does not feel very speedy to the Kirk family", they had "no choice but obviously to accept the schedule the court's put forward".

Nieman added: "The family has waited long enough already; they should not have to wait any longer for this."

During a five-day preliminary hearing in July, prosecutors outlined their evidence to show probable cause for trial.

This included DNA allegedly tying Robinson to the murder weapon and other items recovered at the scene; confessions he allegedly made to loved ones; and a taped video statement from his roommate and romantic partner, Lance Twiggs.

"There is a mountain of evidence that proves that he is the shooter," prosecutor Ryan McBride told the court in September.

"We know that the defendant is the shooterâ¦ first, because of the video evidence; second, because of the DNA; third, because of his admissions; and fourth, because of circumstantial evidence," McBride said.

Defence attorneys, meanwhile, have cast doubt upon evidence and expert witness credentials in the case.

#93. Judge denies motion to dismiss Lindsay Clancy's murder case

The BBC's Ione Wells on how the Lindsay Clancy trial gripped America - and ultimately fell apart

A Massachusetts judge has denied a motion to dismiss the murder case against Lindsay Clancy, the US woman accused of killing her three children.

The judge's ruling follows her defence team's request to dismiss the case after the 12-person jury in her September trial failed to reach a verdict.

Clancy has not denied killing her children in 2023, but her defence team argues she was suffering from severe postpartum psychosis at the time. Prosecutors say the killings were intentional.

The ruling clears the way for prosecutors to pursue the case. They could reduce the charges in a potential second trial or dismiss them altogether.

Clancy's defence team argued to dismiss the case against her due to double jeopardy - a legal standard that prevents a person from being tried for the same crime twice.

Her lawyers argued that Judge William Sullivan, who presided over the 26-day jury trial last summer, acted prematurely when he declared a mistrial on 4 September, saying that he should have sought a viable alternative.

The five-week trial, its lead-up and aftermath gripped Americans, and put postpartum mental health issues such as psychosis in the spotlight.

In his ruling on Monday, Sullivan said that a retrial would not unconstitutionally subject Clancy to double jeopardy, according to court documents obtained by the BBC.

The possibility that "an unreasonably stubborn or eccentric juror" could be seated is "an unavoidable risk of the jury system", he said.

"Because the court concludes that there was manifest necessity for a mistrial based on the jury's deadlock, a retrial on the murder indictments does not violate Clancy's right not to be placed in double jeopardy."

Sullivan also denied a defence motion to see records of the lone holdout juror in the case and to question the juror under oath.

He said the defence failed to demonstrate that the records were needed for Clancy's defence at any retrial, and that questioning the juror "would have been impermissibly coercive".

The judge has marked 16 November to discuss scheduling a potential second trial. He previously suggested it could begin in March.

The BBC has contacted Clancy's lawyers for comment.

Lindsay Clancy's ex-husband says he forgave her so he would not be 'eaten up inside forever'

Lindsay Clancy's trial puts postpartum psychosis in the spotlight

Prosecutors in the closely watched case alleged that on 24 January 2023, Clancy intentionally strangled all of her children - Cora, 5, Dawson, 3, and Callan, eight months - in their Duxbury, Massachusetts, home.

After weeks of emotional testimony, the jury took a week to deliberate, repeatedly telling the judge that they could not reach a verdict.

The deadlock stemmed from a lone juror who voted to convict Clancy of first-degree murder, while the 11 other jurors intended to acquit Clancy by reason of insanity.

In Massachusetts, as well as in all US criminal murder trials, juries must reach a unanimous decision to convict or acquit a defendant.

Because the jurors failed to unanimously agree on whether the prosecution had proved Clancy was guilty "beyond a reasonable doubt" as required by US law, the judge declared a mistrial on 4 September, which means that she was neither found guilty nor not guilty.

Clancy is currently being housed in a state-run psychiatric hospital and medical facility in Massachusetts.

#92. Beau Lamarre-Condon admits entering Jesse Baird’s house when he wasn’t home and eating Easter egg, murder trial hears

Former police officer tells jury he joked about being ‘white girl crazy’ with friends as lawyer questions him about start of relationship with TV presenter

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Beau Lamarre-Condon has denied misusing his authority as a police officer to access CCTV footage of Jesse Baird or stalking him after their breakup, a court has heard.

But he admitted he entered Baird’s house while he wasn’t home to retrieve belongings, and ate an Easter egg he had bought for the television presenter.

The former police officer is accused of fatally shooting Baird and his new partner, Luke Davies, with his police service weapon in Baird’s Paddington home in Sydney on 19 February 2024.

He has pleaded not guilty to two counts of murder and one charge of aggravated break and enter.

Sitting in the witness box for the fourth day, Lamarre-Condon was asked about CCTV footage of Baird at the Royal Paddington hotel that was found on the former officer’s iCloud account.

During cross-examination on Tuesday, the crown prosecutor Brett Hatfield asked: “You were stalking Jesse Baird and also misusing your authority as a policeman to access CCTV of him, weren’t you?”

But Lamarre-Condon denied this, telling the court he had been working in Sydney’s city centre on 19 May 2023 when Baird invited him to the Royal Paddington hotel.

He said he had bumped into other officers, whose names he didn’t know, at the pub that day investigating a possible assault.

Lamarre-Condon was looking at the venue’s CCTV to see if it captured anyone doing an intentional or unintentional “shoulder charge”, when he saw Baird enter the premises on the screen.

“I remember filming from my camera,” the former officer told the court.

“I did send it to Jesse then made a joking banter of it [like] ‘I didn’t get to see you, but detective Beau’s on the case’, and sent it to him.”

Hatfield alleged this was a “deliberately false explanation” but Lamarre-Condon disagreed.

Earlier, Lamarre-Condon was shown screenshots he had taken of Baird’s Instagram story after their breakup in April 2023, before Hatfield asked: “Were you stalking Jesse online and in person in this period in May and June 2023?”

Lamarre-Condon responded: “Mr Crown, if you’re going to accuse me of stalking you’re going to have to show my phone records of where I was on these dates.”

“I definitely wasn’t stalking him, I was looking online,” he told the NSW supreme court.

Lamarre-Condon repeatedly said he was the one who had initiated the split, and told the jury this first breakup with Baird was “amicable”.

“We always followed each other,” he told the court. “That’s not stalking. Am I looking at what he posts? Am I a follower? Yes.”

But the court heard he was still upset about the breakup.

“Jesse and I had good times together,” he said. “I did miss that and I miss him as a friend as well, not just as a sexual partner.”

After the split, Lamarre-Condon went to Baird’s home to retrieve his belongings, he told the court.

The former officer claimed he had sent a message that day to ask whether he was going to be home but said: “As I was arriving, he was leaving. I did not ask permission there and then.”

Lamarre-Condon used a key which had been left out to get his clothes, a Polaroid camera and ate an Easter egg he had bought Baird.

“I didn’t steal the Polaroid camera, I purchased that with my money for us to use … that’s not stealing,” he said.

The crown prosecutor referred to previous evidence from Lamarre-Condon’s friend Georgia Cook, who said the former officer told her had been travelling to Baird’s house in his mum’s car and watching the 26-year-old before stealing the camera and consuming the egg.

Lamarre-Condon claimed she had added her own “salt and pepper” to the account.

On Tuesday morning, former officer told the jury about the early days of the relationship, saying he had met up with Baird in October and November 2022 for sexual encounters before the pair began going on romantic dates in December of that year.

Hatfield asked Lamarre-Condon: “You were obsessed with him right from the start?”

Lamarre-Condon denied this, telling the jury: “Jesse Baird could have been a garbage cleaner … I liked Jesse for who he was as a person.”

Hatfield then asked if Lamarre-Condon was “obsessed” with Baird in February and March 2023, the accused responded: “I don’t agree with ‘obsessed’. Smitten? Yes. Obsessed? No.”

Hatfield pointed to messages between Lamarre-Condon and his friends sent around the time the pair began going on dates, in which the officer says: “it sounds crazy, I love him.”

Lamarre-Condon responded: “I say ‘I love you’ to all my friends and to people like that all the time.”

Asked whether he was insecure about the relationship and whether Baird liked him, Lamarre-Condon said his response was “natural” and sometimes occurred when the TV presenter did not reply to his messages straight away.

“I would always joke [with my friends], ‘We’re white girl crazy’,” Lamarre-Condon told the jury.

“It means we should stop being in our head and second-guessing ourselves.”

Lamarre-Condon told the court he had looked through Baird’s phone and used it to unfollow people when the pair went on a brief holiday to a cabin in regional NSW.

“Our relationship was progressing, I just kind of wanted to see where he was at … if he was also messaging other guys romantically … I’m not making excuses for my behaviour.”

Later, when the prosecution attempted to go through messages between Lamarre-Condon and one of his friends, the officer directly addressed Hatfield, telling him: “Mr Crown, I think you’re trying to mislead me here.”

Justice Natalie Adams interjected, directing Lamarre-Condon to “just answer the question”.

“If the crown is misleading you, [the defence lawyer] will object,” she said.

#89. Turkey steel factory blast kills at least 17 people

A fire broke out at the factory following the explosion which some eyewitnesses confused with an earthquake

At least 17 people have died and 12 others have been injured in an explosion at an iron and steel factory near the port city of Izmir in western Turkey.

All those killed and injured were workers at the Habas Demir Celik plant in Aliaga, one of the country's leading steel producers, authorities said. Footage from the scene showed a large fire inside the facility and a towering column of smoke rising into the air.

The local governor said the blast happened around midday after a crane carrying a replacement boiler collapsed, causing materials to fall into a furnace below.

Workplace accidents remain a chronic problem in Turkey, the Turkish Health and Safety Labor Watch, an independent civil society organisation, said.

President Recep Tayyip ErdoÄan originally said the death toll was 16, but the Health Minister, Kemal MemiÅoÄlu, later announced that one of those who had been critically injured had later died, taking the toll to 17.

Eyewitnesses at the scene said the explosion shook the ground, with one person initially believing an earthquake had struck.

In a statement, the company that runs the factory said the accident occurred during scheduled maintenance work on one of the plant's electric arc furnaces.

Izmir Governor SÃ¼leyman Elban told media that some people were believed to still be under the rubble.

Speaking after a cabinet meeting on Monday, ErdoÄan confirmed the deaths and said investigations were under way, while search and rescue operations continued.

At least five people have been arrested, according BBC News's Turkish service, citing local media reports.

The Habas plant is in the Izmir district of Aliaga which is a major port and one of Turkey's main shipbreaking hubs.

#88. Australia to introduce mandatory reporting after OpenAI hack to combat artificial intelligence ‘going rogue’

Government flags criminal law changes to ensure companies can’t argue in future ‘that someone didn’t know the computer was loaded’

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

OpenAI will not face criminal penalties over the Medicare data breach, but the prime minister has flagged changes to Australia’s criminal law to crack down on future breaches by artificial intelligence companies.

Anthony Albanese on Tuesday released the recommendations of a “rapid review” into the June breach of a Medicare statistics reporting service portal administered by Services Australia, but not the findings or the report itself, which he committed to releasing in the future.

A key recommendation is for the Attorney-General’s Department, the Australian federal police and home affairs to consider introducing “penalties for egregious regulatory non-compliance” and a “failure to prevent” offence, which would criminalise developing an AI model that engaged in harmful conduct while failing “to take reasonable steps to prevent that harm”.

The rapid review came in response to an AI agent, developed by OpenAI, accessing data from Services Australia and three other systems in June. The company did not notify the Australian government until mid-September despite becoming aware of the incident itself in August, prompting criticism from Labor and Albanese.

Guardian Australia revealed last month OpenAI used AI to help write the email to notify Australian authorities.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

Albanese said the government would set up a new AI safety regulator and implement six key obligations on AI companies:

Maintaining security controls across all stages of development and deployment.

Identifying and managing serious risks associated with their models.

Providing mandatory notification of incidents to the Australian government rapidly.

Allowing independent evaluation of their models.

Maintaining and publishing safety and security frameworks to ensure good governance, risk management and transparency.

Responding to updated standards and rules as technologies change.

The review recommended the federal government “consider mandatory, time-bound AI safety reporting”, including around AI agents behaving in ways their creators may not have intended.

The home affairs minister, Tony Burke, appearing with Albanese, told reporters: “It should never be the case that there’s an argument that someone didn’t know the computer was loaded.”

“We need to make sure that our laws and our systems keep pace with the technology and also make sure that we’re able to use the technology to find vulnerabilities before others do,” Burke said.

“The mandatory reporting, when artificial intelligence goes rogue, gives us a chance to be able to make sure that when this happens, the reporting occurs and occurs immediately.”

While Albanese did not directly answer a question about whether the OpenAI incident in June would have breached the proposed new laws if they were in place, government sources indicated that the new legislation would address similar future incidents.

“The clear indication is that no information was got that involves people’s personal details,” Albanese said.

“All of that was confirmed essentially … but that doesn’t mean that it wasn’t a serious issue and that in other areas there might be more serious implications.”

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

In a statement, an OpenAI spokesperson said the company was reviewing the recommendations and the government’s proposals.

“We have been in close dialogue with the government and we’ll continue to engage constructively as it develops its proposed legislation,” they said.

A separate OpenAI-led process, which is discussing better coordination between AI developers and the government, is expected to report back by 31 December.

The rapid review made 18 recommendations. Albanese said “we accept all of the recommendations and we’ve commenced work to implement them”.

The AI researcher Toby Walsh said the people in charge of AI companies needed to be criminally liable.

“We’ve tried fining them and we’re not the only ones. These companies are incredibly wealthy and it’s almost impossible to fine them enough,” Walsh said.

The Greens leader, David Shoebridge, branded Tuesday’s announcement a “capitulation” to the AI industry, claiming the reforms would not do enough to change behaviour. He said the government should have committed to implementing stronger changes rather than the recommendation to “consider” them.

“Safety keeps coming second to corporate investment, and this review locks that priority in by tying every AI safety effort to bringing in more AI money and more datacentres,” Shoebridge claimed.

“Being a world leader in AI doesn’t mean being a doormat to big tech. It means being prepared to regulate it, to protect Australians and to make sure corporate profits don’t come at the expense of everyone else.”

The shadow home affairs minister, Jonno Duniam, said the Coalition backed the announcement. He said “AI is too important to become an unnecessarily partisan political contest”, signalling an “enduring and multi-partisan approach” to the technology.

#85. Qantas to outsource 850 local jobs with most roles moving to India

Qantas CEO Vanessa Hudson apologised for the "hard news" of 850 job cuts

Qantas will outsource 850 local corporate jobs to consulting giant Accenture with most roles moving to India as the airline fast-tracks AI investment.

In an internal email to staff on Tuesday, the Australian company's CEO Vanessa Hudson said "this is hard news for some and I'm sorry for the uncertainty it brings".

The job cuts will begin mid-2027, she said, and start with finance, procurement, people and marketing roles shifting "mostly" to teams in India and "some in Australia" as the firm seeks to modernise its IT systems.

Hudson said the "scale and speed of change in technology and AI is something none of us has seen in our lifetime", and it was in talks with unions and staff on the plans.

The move comes days after the airline provided a market update, flagging fare increases and flight cuts amid higher fuel costs.

On its new multi-year deal with Accenture, Hudson said it was about "modernising" the airline's IT systems to become "more efficient".

Qantas spends more than AU$1bn (US$691m; Â£514m) a year on technology, she said, and the company's back-end systems needed to be upgraded.

"Like every airline, we run on technology built up over decades for a different era, including more than 1,000 legacy applications that were never designed to work together," she said.

The deal with Accenture means "we will redesign and simplify parts of how our corporate functions work and reduce duplication", Hudson said, so staff can "spend more time on work that creates value for our customers and the business".

The company said that "in time", the work that Accenture takes over will be "transformed further with automation and AI".

Staff affected by the job cuts will be redeployed "where possible", the firm added, including new roles in Australia.

Qantas - which employs about 28,000 staff in Australia and another 4,000 globally - said thousands of its staff already use AI in their daily work.

The BBC has contacted the Australian Services Union for comment.

#84. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#83. David Jones’s finances were reviewed and restructuring flagged months before it fell into administration

Department store group kept buying new products and fell months behind in payments to some brands despite discussions with administrators

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

David Jones’s administrators have revealed they reviewed its finances six months ago and discussed restructuring the retailer three months before it fell into administration.

The new timeline raises questions over the department store group’s decision to keep buying new products and fall months behind in payments to some brands before it revealed last Thursday it could not pay its debts.

The process could leave unsecured creditors with substantial losses, with unpaid product suppliers and shoppers holding unredeemed gift vouchers most at risk.

The McGrathNicol administrators invited David Jones’s creditors, which also include employees and landlords, to attend a 17 November meeting in its first circular on Tuesday.

McGrathNicol’s team said it had been engaged by the lawyers for David Jones’s owners, Anchorage Capital, as early as April 2026 to analyse some of the department store group’s financial information, though it said it did not directly advise the group or review its ability to pay debts.

By July, David Jones’s group director, Simon Woodhouse, and its biggest lender, Hilco, met McGrathNicol to discuss David Jones’s financial performance, “potential restructuring” and to form a working group to regularly discuss the issues.

But the administrators said Woodhouse left the meeting when the “potential restructuring” was discussed and no working group was formed.

At the same time, David Jones was putting small suppliers on tiered and extended payment terms, with brands forced to accept ever-deeper discounts to get faster payments, according to several businesses contacted by Guardian Australia.

McGrathNicol said it did not discuss David Jones’s dire financial position again until 22 October, when Woodhouse asked them to help plan a for potential voluntary administration.

It said Woodhouse and others held another five meetings in the following fortnight before a series of briefings last Wednesday, the day before the administration was announced, as the company negotiated with its landlords and prepared for collapse.

McGrathNicol said David Jones Group paid it $1m upfront that day to run the administration. It told creditors it expected to access another $3m to $4m of funding.

The first creditors’ meeting will take place on 17 November, McGrathNicol said.

A second meeting is typically required six weeks later but McGrathNicol said it would apply to have that delayed another three months, or to early April 2027, to give it more time to sell the business or raise more funds.

The letter did not offer more detail on David Jones’s financial situation, or how long it had been insolvent. McGrathNicol said it had asked the company’s directors to prepare a report on their business, property and financial circumstances.

FTI Consulting, which is running David Jones’s day-to-day business during the administration process, on Monday said the company had remained fully operational.

Anchorage Capital, David Jones and Hilco were contacted for comment on Tuesday night.

#80. Victorian Labor’s ‘unforgivable’ preferences decision risks Liberal-One Nation government, Greens warn

Exclusive: Ellen Sandell says Labor’s decision not to back progressives in key inner-seat could hand power to right-wing coalition in close election

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The Victorian Greens say Labor’s “frankly unforgivable” decision not to preference the minor party in a key inner-city seat could hand the upcoming election to a Liberal-One Nation coalition.

The party’s leader, Ellen Sandell, lambasted Labor’s decision on Tuesday to run an open ticket in Prahran, an inner Melbourne seat currently held by the Liberals’ Rachel Westaway on a tiny 1.3% margin.

It means Labor’s how-to-vote material will encourage voters to place their candidate, Meghan Hopper, first on the ballot but will not direct them how to allocate their remaining preferences. Instead, the remaining boxes on its how-to-vote material will probably remain blank.

Labor preferences are key to the Greens cinching back the seat, which they lost in a 2025 byelection.

The Greens received a 13.4% swing at the poll, with the result influenced by several factors, including Labor’s decision not to field a candidate, low voter turnout, a well-resourced campaign urging voters to put them last and unfavourable preference flows from other candidates. This included the former Labor MP Tony Lupton, who contested the byelection as an independent.

Sandell told Guardian Australia Labor was “showing its true colours” by running an open ticket, as it could inadvertently help the Liberals and One Nation not only secure the seat but also government.

“After months of scaremongering about a Liberal-One Nation government, Labor is now using its preferences to help elect one,” she said.

“This could be one of the most consequential elections in the state’s history. The result may come down to a single seat, and a divisive party like One Nation could hold the balance of power.

“It is frankly unforgivable that Labor would do a dodgy back-room deal to help elect a Liberal-One Nation government – and it’s something that Victorian voters will not easily forget.”

Sandell said Labor voters would be also “shocked” to see the party refusing to preference a “local, progressive Greens woman in Prahran”, Angelica Di Camillo, who also ran at the byelection.

Labor is also running an open ticket in the neighbouring seat of Caulfield, held by the Liberals’ David Southwick on a 2.1% margin. But it is expected Southwick will see a swing in his favour and comfortably retain the seat.

It is understood the local campaigns in Prahran and Caulfield requested the open ticket arrangement, which is consistent with the position Labor took at the 2025 federal election in the overlapping seat of Macnamara, held by Josh Burns, amid the height of the Israel-Hamas war in Gaza.

A Labor spokesperson said: “Our message is simple: put a 1 next to Labor candidates everywhere. We’re fighting for majority government.”

Caulfield is home to Victoria’s largest Jewish community, which also extends into St Kilda East in the Prahran electorate.

On Tuesday, the Liberals and Nationals also confirmed they would exchange preferences with One Nation, with the opposition leader, Jess Wilson, telling reporters how-to-vote cards for Coalition candidates would instruct voters to put Labor, the Greens and the Victorian Socialists “at the bottom of the ticket, and all other parties above that”.

Just hours after Wilson confirmed the position, One Nation moved to disendorse its Preston candidate, Franco Guardiani, over tweets, including one that described Hitler as the “GOAT”, an acronym which stands for greatest of all time.

The Victorian Liberal Senator Jane Hume told ABC’s Afternoon Briefing Guardiani’s tweets were “vile” and confirmed he would be last on the party’s how-to-vote material in the electorate.

#77. Australia considers criminal penalties for rogue AI in wake of OpenAI hack

PM Anthony Albanese says the law needs to catch up with a "fast-changing world"

Australia plans to create a new AI safety regulator and has flagged criminal penalties for tech companies who do not comply, months after an OpenAI hack.

In September, Australia's Prime Minister Anthony Albanese revealed that a rogue OpenAI agent had "infiltrated" a government health website and other systems, and criticised the company for taking months to tell them about the hack.

On Tuesday, he released the 18 recommendations made after a "rapid review" into the breaches including new safety and security standards for AI companies such as mandatory reporting of hacks.

AI brings opportunities, he said, but Australia must prepare for the "new risks" from this "rapidly advancing technology".

"The law needs to get ahead of and, in some cases, make sure that it catches up with, what is a very fast changing world," Albanese said.

One of the recommendations suggests changes to criminal law to have a standalone "failure to prevent" offence .

This would criminalise instances where a company develops "an AI model that engages in harmful conduct" and "fails to take reasonable steps to prevent that harm".

The review also recommended "mandatory, time-bound AI safety reporting" of hacks.

This point addresses what Albanese had earlier called his "disappointment" in the months-long delay for OpenAI to tell his government about the breach.

In June, a statistics portal containing "non-sensitive" data from Australia's universal healthcare scheme Medicare was hacked by a rogue OpenAI agent.

OpenAI said it only learnt of the breach in August while reviewing "misaligned model activity" and emailed a general inbox of an Australian government agency on 10 September.

It took several more days before the email was escalated to Australia's cybersecurity centre, with a message then sent to a government minister ahead of the prime minister being told.

Albanese said he spoke to OpenAI boss Sam Altman and raised "Australia's extreme concern about this incident", with Altman acknowledging there were "issues with protocols" at OpenAI.

Days after the hacks were revealed, OpenAI delayed its rollout of a new AI model, citing safety concerns.

Why did an OpenAI system hack Australia's health system - and can it be stopped in the future?

OpenAI scraps rollout of new model over safety concerns

#76. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#72. Oxford museum returns ancestral Naga skulls to India

A delegation of 30 people had travelled to Oxford from India's Nagaland

The University of Oxford has returned the remains of 39 people taken from the Naga community during British colonial rule in India.

A delegation of around 30 people from Nagaland, a mountainous state in north-eastern India, attended a ceremony at Oxford's Pitt Rivers Museum on Monday to mark the handover.

The remains, most of which are skulls, will be taken back to India and given what Naga tribal leaders have described as a "respectful rest". There are plans to bury them under a joint memorial in a park in Nagaland.

Museum director Laura Van Broekhoven described it as "a historic moment that will be part of stories told to next generations".

She said the remains were finally on their way home after overcoming "so much red tape everywhere".

The human remains were displayed at the Oxford museum for decades before being removed from public view in 2020 as part of what it described as a "decolonisation process".

The museum formally handed over the remains and signed an agreement with Naga leaders on Monday

The Pitt Rivers Museum holds more than 6,000 objects associated with Naga culture, believed to be the largest such collection in the world. Most were acquired during British colonial rule in India and Myanmar (the former Burma).

The museum's website lists 219 items as human remains.

These were added to the museum's collection between 1910 and 1940 and mostly collected by two colonial administrators who were anthropologists.

The museum's website, external mentions that the objects collected during this period were "frequently confiscated during punitive military expeditions, rather than freely given".

During their visit to Oxford, the Naga delegation also met descendants of one of the anthropologists involved in collecting the remains.

"The manner in which these human remains were taken away from our land leaves much for us to speculate," one of the tribal leaders, Thejao Vihienuo, told AFP.

"I suspect that nobody would have given the skull of his father or his grandfather as a gift to another person."

Anthropologists say that the Naga people once practised head hunting, severing the heads of enemies as trophies. But they also kept the heads of their own chiefs at the centre of villages.

The Pitt Rivers Museum, founded in the 19th Century, has previously returned human remains to indigenous communities in Australia, New Zealand and the US.

The latest handover to India concerns only the 39 ancestral remains and comes after a visit by a Naga delegation to Oxford in 2025, when it formally requested their return.

The museum had announced in September that the remains would be returned to India.

#71. New Zealand election coalition talks will likely take weeks – as it happened

This blog is closed, you can read our report on the New Zealand election here

Thanks for following the blog tonight. The election has ended without a clear winner. Here are the top news lines from what will go down as one of New Zealand’s closest elections in many decades.

Christopher Luxon’s National party and its right-leaning coalition partners are on the brink of a wafer-thin majority that could allow it to eke out a return to power.

The left-wing bloc, led by Labour’s Chris Hipkins, fell short of a majority.

Neither Luxon or Hipkins claimed victory or conceded defeat. Both acknowledged an expected wait before a governing coalition is negotiated.

A centrist newcomer, the Opportunity party, has entered parliament, polling above the 5% threshold. They could potentially be a kingmaker in a coalition.

Final results are expected on 27 November. Coalition talks will likely take weeks.

Hundreds of thousands of special votes are yet to be counted and could change the shape of parliament.

Saturday figures showed there could be a 130-seat parliament, with 66 seats needed for a majority.

Results suggested a coalition of National, Act and NZ First would hold 67 seats.

The 67 included 42 for National, 13 for Act and 12 for NZ First.

The left-bloc totalled 56 seats: Labour 35, Greens 16 and 5 for Te Pāti Māori.

Opportunity is projected to gain 7 seats.

Over the coming days and weeks we will get a sense of the magnitude of Qiulae Wong and the Opportunity party’s success tonight. Opportunity are currently projected to claim seven seats in parliament.

Wong has said that if she held the tiebreaker power, she was open to negotiating with either a conservative or a left-wing government and would deal first with whichever major party won the most votes.

The right-wing bloc might not need her, and Luxon has ruled out governing with Opportunity. Analysts say, however, that both major party leaders would likely answer the phone if Wong held the only path to power.

You can read more about the rise of Opportunity here, in this piece from Michelle Duff.

A couple of pics of Christopher Luxon as he arrived to speak to supporters tonight.

On the current count, the Greens are celebrating a best-ever result of what looks to be 16 seats in parliament.

With 0.56% support, Te Pāti Māori will be stoked with its result tonight, writes Jamie Tahana.

It seems odd, but the Māori party has spent the election deliberately asking people not to give them their party vote. Instead, they asked for electorate votes only.

Just a reminder, seven of the seats in New Zealand’s parliament are reserved for representatives of Māori voters – you can read more about them here.

The party’s co-leaders, Rawiri Waititi and Debbie Ngarewa-Packer, have comfortably won their respective seats of Waiariki and Te Tai Hauauru; Oriini Kaipara swept the Auckland seat of Tamaki Makaurau; Hana Rawhiti Maipi-Clarke, who hit international stardom for her haka in parliament, has cruised across the line in Hauraki-Waikato; while Aperahama Edwards holds a narrow lead over the Greens’ Hūhana Lydon in the far North seat of Te Tai Tokerau.

That’s five seats. But with a party vote so low, they will be five overhang seats.

Still, after a term that has seen plenty of in-fighting in Te Pāti Māori, there are signs of frustration in the Māori electorate, with Waititi and Ngarewa-Packer seeing significantly reduced majorities.

The Greens have fallen short of gaining their first ever Māori electorate, but across the seats party vote has risen to about 40%, making the party a significant force in Māori politics.

Labour, having been punished by the Māori electorate in 2023, has improved, particularly in terms of party vote, and has claimed a new MP in Mananui Ramsden, who won the country’s largest electorate, Te Tai Tonga.

So, hundreds of thousands of special votes are going to potentially change where things stand right now.

In his speech tonight, Luxon said 300,000 special votes were yet to be counted. Those votes, which will be counted and known by 27 November, typically sway left.

In the 2023 election there 600,000 special votes, and National dropped from 50 seats in the preliminary count to 48 seats.

In a 130-seat parliament, in which 66 seats are needed to govern, this is where things stand with 97% of the vote counted. However, special votes are still to be counted and the lay of the land will not be clear until 27 November.

Labour 35, Greens 16, Te Pāti Māori 5 = 56

National 29.3%, Labour 29.2%, Greens 13.3%, Act 11%, NZ First 9.8%, Opportunity 6%, Te Pāti Māori 0.6%.

Jamie Tahana has this hot-take on the Luxon speech:

It was a tough line to tread for a speech: National is the biggest party on current results – 29.30% – but Luxon has also led the party to one of its most unpopular points, only 0.1% ahead of Labour. Luxon has also lost some of his most senior and experienced MPs, which he conceded required “some tough phone calls”.

Luxon said that because of the closeness of the result, and with special votes still to be counted, caretaker conventions will apply.

“For now, I want to tell New Zealanders that well established caretaker conventions apply … The daily business of government will continue.”

Caretaker convention basically means that while a new government is being formed, the incumbent government is in “caretaker” mode, which means it will carry on the normal business of governing.

Jamie Tahana notes that for Luxon to continue as prime minister, he will have to cobble together a three- or four-party coalition, from a weakened position compared to 2023.

In his speech, Luxon said: “Negotiations and compromises are going to be required … A number of races are still too close to call.”

Jamie Tahana has some more from the Luxon speech:

Luxon called the election “too close to call”, and said “meaningful negotiations” for a new coalition government will start after the final results are known on 27 November.

“It looks like an MMP special,” he said. “It’s heartening to see that so far National is once again the largest party in parliament and we have a path to forming the next government.”

Luxon concedes result unlikely tonight, saying that meaningful negotiations will begin after final results on 27 November.

Luxon arrived to a crowd of about 700 raucous supporters chanting “three more years”. He was flanked by his wife Amanda and two children, waved and grinned as he descended a stairwell flanked in blue light.

A heavy music track - which could not be immediately identified through Shazam - thrummed as he walked down a long aisle, hugging supporters.

When he took the podium, Luxon conceded the election was “too close to call” but the crowd cheered as he announced National had secured the highest party count.

He went on to acknowledge Labour leader Chris Hipkins, and admonished supporters who booed Quilae Wong and Opportunity, saying it was “not the kiwi way”.

Luxon is also paying tribute to those high-ranking National MPs who find themselves out of a job tonight: Nicola Willis, Paul Goldsmith and Gerry Brownlee. “I want to thank you … for your years of service to our party”.

Luxon has called tonight’s result “an MMP special” and says “on the numbers so far, National is once again the largest party in parliament”.

It will be interesting to see what Luxon has to say. How will he frame the vote count and National’s performance?

Christopher Luxon is high-fiving and hugging supporters. He’ll be speaking soon.

#67. Trump says more US attacks on Iran 'highly unlikely'

Trump: "Iran militarily is effectively over"

Donald Trump has said "Iran militarily is effectively over", and said that there is "positive momentum" behind negotiations to end the Middle East conflict.

"We're just keeping it the status quo," the US president said on Monday. "And we're doing that really at the behest of countries that are their neighbours."

Trump's comments came after he met the Qatari prime minister. Qatar is a US ally in the Gulf region, and one of Iran's neighbours, which has been a key mediator in efforts to end the war.

Addressing reporters in the White House press briefing room, Trump said that renewed US attacks on Iran were "highly unlikely" but that the US was "locked and loaded" should the circumstances change.

Iranian officials have repeatedly expressed doubts about Trump's prior comments about resolving the conflict.

This is not the first time that Trump has said the Iran war is "militarily" over, but the circumstances for him are different now.

The president has hinted at de-escalation in Iran after a bruising set of midterm election results for the Republicans last week, which senior figures including Vice-President JD Vance blamed on the Iran war.

Ahead of the midterms, Trump had said US forces conducted what he described as "minor strikes" on an Iranian island in the Strait of Hormuz. However, in an interview with 60 Minutes that aired on Sunday, the president said that he did not currently see "any reason" to escalate the conflict.

Responding to a question over why he would not officially declare an end to the war, Trump said that some of the Gulf allies could still face Iranian aggression.

"We have some great allies there and we have to make sure they're not hurt," he said, suggesting that the US naval blockade on Iranian ports was helping to make the region more secure.

Global oil prices, which have fallen sharply since the midterms, dipped further after Trump's comments.

On Tuesday afternoon in Asia, Brent crude was more than 1% lower at $91.50 (Â£52) while US-traded was down by 1.2% at $85.82.

Could Democrats in Congress end the war in Iran?

Trump hosted Qatari Prime Minister Sheikh Mohammed bin Abdul Rahman Al Thani in the Oval Office earlier on Monday, saying they had an "excellent conversation" about negotiations to end the war.

In a post on X, the Qatari leader said he held "positive discussions" with Trump and Vance on "reaching a deal and bringing peace to the region".

Efforts to broker a peace between Washington and Tehran have largely been held indirectly through mediators since the war first broke out following joint US-Israeli strikes in February.

Saudi Arabia, Qatar and the United Arab Emirates are among the US's Gulf allies that have been hit by Iranian strikes over the course of the war, in addition to the threat of strikes that have led to major air travel disruptions in the region.

Republican congressman Thomas Massie plans to force a vote in the House of Representatives this week to impeach Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth

Post-election recriminations over the US war in Iran are already placing pressure on members of the Trump administration.

Republican congressman Thomas Massie launched a strong attack against Trump's Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth on Monday over the war in Iran, requesting a vote in the House of Representatives to impeach him.

Trump has backed Hegseth, who says he is "not worried" about the looming impeachment threats, calling Massie's effort a "sham".

While the move is unlikely to succeed with Republicans still in control of Congress until January, Democrats have expressed willingness to impeach Hegseth once the new session begins and they have control of both the House and the Senate.

#64. NZ election 2026: New Zealand faces weeks of uncertainty with results ‘too close to call’

National party will be largest in parliament but ultimate shape of next government won’t be settled for some time

New Zealand could be facing a weeks-long wait to know the shape of its next government, after its nail-biting election failed to hand a decisive victory to either the incumbent rightwing coalition or opposition left bloc.

On preliminary results, the centre-right National party, led by the prime minister, Christopher Luxon, managed to secure enough seats to govern alongside its traditional coalition partners, the libertarian Act and populist New Zealand First parties.

But with some seats won by very slim margins, and special votes yet to be counted, that success is precarious, resulting in an election too close to call until official results are released on 27 November.

Special votes include overseas votes and ballots cast outside a voter’s electorate. In 2023, 600,000 such votes were made, 21% of the total. These votes tend to favour the left – in 2023, special votes resulted in Te Pāti Māori (the Māori party) and the Greens gaining three seats between them.

With nearly 100% of the regular votes counted on Saturday night, National secured 42 seats, Act 13 and New Zealand First 12. The combined 67-seat majority is wafer thin, with the left bloc just three seats behind.

Speaking to a room full of the party faithful at Auckland’s Viaduct events centre just before midnight, Luxon said the results were “too close to call” and the special votes would need to be counted before negotiations could begin.

National was still the largest party in parliament, he said, and had “a pathway to forming the next government”. National pipped Labour by 0.02% in the party vote.

Labour, led by Chris Hipkins, secured 35 seats, while its traditional coalition partners the Greens won 16 and Te Pāti Māori five. Even with the seven seats from the centrist newcomer party, Opportunity, the left bloc is three seats short of forming a government.

Before a rowdy crowd of supporters at the Cosmopolitan Club in Upper Hutt, north of Wellington, Hipkins joked that New Zealand had spoken but “it may take a bit longer to work out what they’ve said”.

“As things stand tonight, it is clear that we are not going to know the shape of our next government for a little while longer yet,” Hipkins said, promising that Labour would get “straight to work” should the final count land in its favour.

The collapse of the two major parties, amid a sluggish economy and stubbornly high ⁠inflation, is feeding record support for the smaller parties, including Act, New Zealand First and the Greens. It is also helped propel Opportunity into parliament for the first time.

Opinion polls in the lead-up to the election had Opportunity placed to become the kingmaker. At this stage, the right bloc does not need the party to govern, but if special votes change the landscape, its predicted role could come to fruition.

Currently at 5.9%, the centrist party has met the threshold needed to enter parliament, a historic first. Since New Zealand adopted the mixed-member proportional (MMP) electoral system in 1996, no new party has been able to meet that threshold – all the other minor parties have been born from MPs or groups splintering from larger parties.

The party’s leader, Qiulae Wong, told her supporters in Auckland she was “stoked”.

“It’s looking very promising for us,” she said. “Let’s just see how the rest of the votes fall.”

In New Zealand, voters cast two votes: one for an electorate MP and one for a party. The system is meant to produce a parliament that reflects each party’s share of the nationwide vote.

However, if New Zealanders vote for more electorate MPs from a particular party than their party vote would entitle them to, they get to keep the extra MPs in what is called an overhang.

National won more seats that its party support allows for, meaning it could gain an extra five seats to make up the balance.

That will be good news for its path to power, but comes with some significant trade-offs for the party. Four of its most senior ministers, including Nicola Willis, Paul Goldsmith, Chris Bishop and Gerry Brownlee, will not return to parliament because they did not win a seat, or the party didn’t win enough votes to bring in list-only MPs.

On the other side of the house, Te Pāti Māori picked up five overhang seats.

With all the extra seats, there could be 130 in the next parliament – a record under MMP – which means 65 seats are needed to form a coalition.

The country faces a two-week wait until special votes are announced, followed by the possibility of weeks of negotiations. Luxon said caretaker government conventions would apply in the meantime.

“For now, I want to tell New Zealanders that well-established caretaker conventions apply … The daily business of government will continue.”

#62. French wartime resistance heroine Solange Alexandre dies aged 104

Solange Alexandre twice turned down the LÃ©gion d'Honneur before relenting in 2024

One of France's last Resistance heroes from World War Two has died at the age of 104. Solange Alexandre was also a rare woman who, with her future husband, created and commanded her own unit of anti-German fighters.

After serving as a Resistance courier from 1941, three years later she founded the Pas-de-Boeuf network near the town of RuillÃ©-sur-Loir.

With some 30 men under her orders, she organised sabotage operations, sheltered Allied airmen and, after the Allied landings in Normandy on D-Day in 1944, joined the fight against the occupying German forces.

President Emmanuel Macron was among many to pay tribute to Alexandre, after her death was announced at a nursing home in Le Mans.

"She said she only ever did her duty. But her duty was our freedom. France will never forget. Thank you," Macron posted on social media.

Born in 1922 in the Sarthe department of western France, Solange Alexandre, nÃ©e Dubuisson, grew up in a patriotic family that had suffered in World War One.

"My mother lost her first husband in the war, and my father was wounded in 1917. The words homeland and honour were very important to us. They were in my blood," she recalled in her memoirs.

In June 1940 she heard on the radio the appeal from General Charles de Gaulle in London, calling on the French not to give up the struggle against Germany. She had never heard of him, but that did not matter.

"From that moment I knew I had to join up," she wrote.

Some months later she was recruited by a friend of her father's â a Tunisian-born French army veteran called SaÃ¯d Belhaffaf, who had joined the Resistance.

Using the code-name Corlay, she criss-crossed the region around Le Mans by bicycle, gathering information and passing on messages.

"There were no mobile phones back then, and no private cars either because of the lack of petrol. The bicycle was king," she said in an interview two years ago.

In 1943 she met her first husband Guy DÃ©liot, who had deserted from the French gendarmerie after refusing to arrest some Jews.

The following April, with the Allied invasion approaching, they decided to take to the maquis, in other words live clandestinely in the forested hills, supplied with food by local farmers.

Their group consisted of deserters from German forced labour programmes, as well as survivors from another Resistance outfit that had been destroyed by the enemy. There were also two airmen, one American and one Canadian.

"Twice the Gestapo came for me, and twice I slipped through their hands," she later recalled.

After taking part in the liberation of Tours in September 1944, she ended the war with the rank of sub-lieutenant in the French Interior Forces (FFI).

Later she worked as a teacher and followed her husband on his many postings as an officer of the gendarmerie in French overseas territories. He was killed in 1960 in Martinique, and in 1981 she remarried.

Twice Solange Alexandre turned down the LÃ©gion d'Honneur, saying that she had only done her duty and that others were more worthy.

On receiving the award she said: "It is a great pleasure and a great honour. I am very proud â even if it is only a few weeks, or a few months, before I die."

#57. Israeli forces kill Palestinian boy in West Bank, health ministry says

Israeli forces carried out a raid in the Old City of Nablus, near Burqa, on Sunday

Israeli forces have shot dead a 14-year-old Palestinian boy during an operation in the occupied West Bank, Palestinian officials say.

Moataz Daghlas was killed on Monday evening in the village of Burqa, north-west of Nablus, according to the Palestinian health ministry.

The Israeli military said soldiers identified "several terrorists who intended to hurl stones" in an area where stones had previously been thrown at Israeli vehicles. The soldiers fired towards them "to remove the threat", it added.

The Palestinian Red Crescent said its teams treated two people with gunshot wounds, but that Israeli forces blocked their access to a third wounded person and detained three volunteer paramedics.

Moataz Daghlas's relatives said Israeli authorities were withholding his body.

Meanwhile, Palestinian news agency Wafa identified the two Palestinians wounded during the raid as a 17-year-old child, who it said was shot in the abdomen and pelvis, and a 19-year-old man, who was shot in the hand.

According to the UN, at least 98 Palestinians, including 22 children, have been killed by Israeli forces or settlers in the West Bank since the start of the year.

Six Israelis, both security forces and civilians, have been killed by Palestinians in the West Bank over the same period, it says.

The rules of engagement for Israeli forces were reportedly broadened after the deadly Hamas-led attacks on Israel on 7 October 2023, allowing soldiers increased discretion to use lethal force against a perceived threat.

In June, the UN Human Rights Office in the occupied Palestinian territory said it had verified the killing of 241 Palestinian children by Israeli security forces and settlers in the West Bank since then.

Most of the children were teenage boys and, in many cases, Israeli authorities accused them of having engaged in stone-throwing as justification, it found.

The UN office said its assessment was that none of the children killed while involved in confrontations with Israeli forces had posed an imminent threat to life, which it said was the only legal justification for the use of lethal force in the West Bank under international law.

#55. Uyghurs facing deportation by Thailand say they will be ‘like slaves’ in China

Activists fear Bangkok will give in to pressure from Beijing despite reports of torture and human rights abuses against the mostly muslim ethnic minority

The deportation of two more Uyghurs to China from Thailand would put their lives in grave danger, activists have said, with one of the men saying he would be “like a slave” if he was sent back.

Last year, Thai authorities forcibly deported 40 Uyghur men to China despite pleas from the men themselves and the UN. The US and Canada had reportedly offered to resettle them, but Bangkok appeared to want to avoid upsetting Beijing. The UN has said the returnees’ whereabouts remain unknown.

Now, two more men fear becoming the latest victims of Beijing’s repressive crackdown against the largely Muslim ethnic group, condemned by the UN as a potential crime against humanity.

The girlfriend of one of the men said her boyfriend, whose name is being withheld for security reasons, told the Guardian he wanted to go “anywhere else but China”.

Human rights groups said two Uyghur men were at risk of deportation after recently being transferred to an immigration detention centre and they feared Thai authorities may once again give in to pressure from Beijing.

Elaine Pearson, Asia director at Human Rights Watch, called for the men to be allowed to travel to a safe third country. “Previous Thai governments have kept Uyghurs in inhumane detention for years and then complied with Beijing’s demands to send them to China, where they were forcibly disappeared,” she said.

The men had been serving a seven-year prison sentence for offences related to an attempted escape from immigration detention in January 2020. They were part of a group of more than 200 Uyghur men, women and children who were apprehended by Thai police in 2014 near the border with Malaysia after they fled from China.

Since 2014, women and children in the group have been released to Turkey, while the men have been deported to China, in separate batches, attracting criticism from the United Nations and the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio.

Uyghurs, a Turkic minority mainly from the Xinjiang region in China, have been subjected to decades of oppressive and discriminatory treatment from the Chinese authorities. This has included the incarceration of hundreds of thousands of people in detention camps aimed at stamping out their cultural identity.

Beijing has defended its policies in Xinjiang as being aimed at poverty alleviation and counter-terrorism.

In July, Marzeyya Kasem’s (who asked to be referred to by her Islamic alias) boyfriend wrote to her in a letter that he would be “like a slave” if he was sent back to China. “Sometimes I thought why I’m a human being. What I do wrong against China … no want to go back to China,” he wrote. She shared the letter with a Thai rights group.

The two men facing imminent deportation reportedly pleaded their case to Thailand’s office of the National Human Rights Commission on Wednesday.

Relatives of Uyghurs have said the Chinese government was “paranoid about Uyghurs going to a safe country in large numbers and living together”, and preferred to have them deported.

It is not clear whether China has issued a formal extradition request for the two men. But Chaipruak Doungprapat, Thailand’s national security council chief, told reporters that the men, along with three other Uyghurs who are still in prison, had arrest warrants outstanding in China.

Some have questioned whether criminal warrants could be credible considering that at least one of them, Kasem’s boyfriend, was a minor when he left China. “If they are really wanted for a crime in China, why didn’t China immediately issue a [Interpol] red notice [when they were detained in 2014]?” asked Kasem.

Kasem believes that because Thailand was criticised for last year’s mass deportation, this time the authorities were trying to establish a more credible legal basis for the move.

A spokesperson for China’s foreign affairs ministry said: “The cooperation between China and Thailand in combating cross-border illegal and criminal activities such as smuggling is consistent with the laws of the two countries, international law and international practice.”

A spokesperson for Thailand’s foreign affairs ministry declined to comment.
